WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
 
ES-1 
 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
 
The Wicomico County Water and Sewer Master Plan (WC-WSMP) recommends that the County 
Council legislatively amend County Code Chapter 97 Urban Service Districts and alternatively 
create a Water Division within the Department of Public Works to provide water related utility 
services inclusive of full operations, maintenance, and ownership.    The Master Plan envisions the 
probability that the Water Division will grow over time and eventually warrant becoming a 
standalone Department of Water within County government.   
 
The Wicomico County Water and Sewer Master Plan considered existing water and sewer utility 
infrastructure within the eight incorporated cities and towns and developed the Master Plan with 
the flexibility to allow incorporated cities and towns to expand their water and sewer utility 
infrastructure in general accordance with their municipal comprehensive plans or seek cooperative 
win-win agreements with the County for mutual benefit.   
 
The WC-WSMP assessed rural villages and subdivision communities throughout the County lying 
outside municipal growth boundaries and projected long-term growth in strong alignment with the 
2017 County Comprehensive Plan.    The Plan identified twelve (12) potential water and/or sewer 
utility service districts as follows: 
▪ Mardela Springs 
▪ Whitehaven Rural Village 
▪ Parsonsburg Rural Village and nearby subdivision communities 
▪ Coulbourn Mill Road area subdivision communities 
▪ Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin Rural Villages 
▪ Nanticoke Road area subdivision communities 
▪ Riverside Road Extended area 
▪ Quantico Rural Village 
▪ Allen Rural Village 
▪ Powellville Rural Village 
▪ East Delmar Community Service District 
▪ East Wicomico Community Service District 
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 3 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
ES-2 
 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
The WC-WSMP recommends that the County Council begin legislative process as soon as practical 
for the first five proposed districts listed below. 
▪ Mardela Springs 
▪ Whitehaven Rural Village 
▪ Parsonsburg Rural Village and nearby subdivision communities 
▪ Coulbourn Mill Road area subdivision communities 
▪ Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin Rural Villages 
 
The WC-WSMP identified a significant number of County homeowners, lying within planned City 
growth areas currently experiencing hardships with failing onsite sewage disposal systems and/or 
individual wells.  Based upon the Comprehensive Plan developed by the municipalities, approved 
by the Maryland Department of Planning (MDP) and not opposed by the County; the WC-WSMP 
recommends to the County Council to take a supportive role regarding annexations and extensions 
of urban water and sewer utility services.    
 
The focus of the WC-WSMP was threefold: 
➢ Safeguard public health,  
➢ Prevent degradation of our water environment, and  
➢ Enrich the economic well-being of Wicomico County.  
 
This focus guided the WC-WSMP in developing the best-balanced solution to meet the 
technological, managerial, and financial needs for providing rural centralized utility systems for 
Wicomico County.   The graphic below exemplifies that balance and creating overlap for to 
achieve best outcome. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The WC-WSMP is an engineering planning document that provides sufficient technical and cost 
information for the seeking financial funding through State and Federal grant and loan funding

WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
 
ES-3 
programs.   Furthermore, the Plan provides an accounting of the nitrogen loadings entering the 
Chesapeake Bay watershed through onsite septic disposal systems into the groundwater.  This data 
provides the justification for seeking discharge permits to safely dispose into the environment the 
highly treated sewage after its processing and transformation to clean water.    
 
The recommendation to Wicomico County to implement rural centralized utility systems is not 
solely based through the financial lens of affordability.  The recommendation encompasses a more 
holistic perspective inclusive of protecting the economic (assessed) value of properties; protecting 
the public health of affected residents; and reducing the existing negative environmental impact to 
the water resources in the County for the benefit of ALL county residents.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
 
ES-5 
The Guiding Principle and Implementation Action Table provides a list of guiding principles to be 
adopted by Wicomico County by acceptance of the Master Plan and action steps moving forward. 
 
Guiding Principle and Implementation Action 
 
GUIDING PRINCIPLE 
IMPLEMENTATION ACTION 
1 
 
 
Adopt Management-Governance 
Structure 
 
County Council adopts a management-governance 
structure and passes legislative action to amend the 
County Charter/Code, if necessary, based on 
recommendation from County Executive.  
2 
 
Infrastructure system capacities will be 
based on existing flows and controlled 
growth projections in conformity with the 
County Comprehensive Plan, and Water 
& Sewer Plan. 
 
As needed, County Council reviews and 
recommends approval of County Comprehensive 
Plan and associated growth capacity. Assumes that 
no legislative action required. 
3 
 
Prohibition of private wastewater 
treatment systems serving more than one 
landowner parcel.  
 
County Council adopts the prohibition, to be 
followed by enacting appropriate enabling 
legislation. 
4 
Policy allowing Private Water Companies 
to provide community water systems as 
an alternative to municipal systems. 
County Council adopts the policy to be followed by 
enacting appropriate enabling legislation 
5 
Planning, Engineering, Construction, 
Operations, and Management of water 
and sewer infrastructure.  
Implementation by County Executive through the 
chosen management structure with narrow defined 
oversight by County Council. 
6 
Technology decisions on type of 
infrastructure (gravity sewers vs pressure 
sewer; treatment technologies; individual 
systems vs community systems) 
Technical engineering decisions approved by 
County Executive Office with County Council 
acceptance. 
7 
Support municipal annexations when the 
alternative offers the best possible 
technological and most cost-effective 
utility option. 
County Council accept annexations when 
recommended by County Executive and jointly 
collaborate with municipalities for implementation.

WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
 
1-1 
Chapter 1:  Master Plan Introduction  
1.1  Introduction 
Wicomico County commissioned the Water and Sewer Master Plan (WC-WSMP) to provide 
information and guidance to the County and its municipal governments and County citizens.  It is 
not a Plan with a road map for the County to take over existing municipal utility systems.  It is not 
a Plan to alter or hinder Comprehensive Plans or growth initiatives by any of the eight (8) 
municipalities within Wicomico County.  This Plan is intended to help solve public health 
problems and ensure protection of the environment while facilitating growth plans articulated in 
the County’s Comprehensive Plan of 2017.  This Plan investigates issues affecting safe drinking 
water and environmentally sound practices for the treatment and disposal of treated wastewater 
(clean water), and attempts to provide a path forward for water and sewer utility services that is 
financially feasible and sustainable.  In the long run, this Master Plan is intended to facilitate and 
accommodate desired growth, enhance economic outcomes, and improve the quality of life in 
Wicomico County.   
 
 A major thrust of this Master Plan is consideration of County residences served by individual 
wells and onsite septic disposal systems (OSDS) that are aging, failing, and increasingly 
impractical or not cost-effective to repair or replace.  For a significant portion of OSDS, the 
recommendation will be connecting to the nearby municipality through a central sewerage 
collection system and halting the use of septic tank systems in these areas.  In some cases, 
connection to a municipal water system which will not only provide safe drinking water but also 
fire protection along with central sewerage will also be recommended.   
 
This Master Plan will create a vision for the installation of central water and sewer systems for the 
non-sewered areas close to existing Cities and Towns, primarily by connection to existing 
municipal systems.  It will also propose central water and/or sewerage systems for the 
unincorporated villages and community subdivisions, some of which may not be constructed for 
decades into the future.    
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 24 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
1-2 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
The current County management-governance approach to water and sewerage utility services, 
known as the Urban Service Commission (USC), contained in Chapter 97 of the County Code, 
will be reviewed for its relevance and practicality to continue.  This plan will also consider the 
creation of alternate management-governance models such as the creation of non-profit public 
agency or authority; or establishing a water department or water division. 
 
As a “Master Plan,” this effort looks more than 50 years into the future and beyond the turn of the 
22nd Century.  The County Executive office and County Council will need to consider the full 
ramifications of directly providing water and sewer utility services to individual citizens, and select 
a management-governance approach.   It is noted that the creation of this Master Plan does include 
conversations with incorporated Cities and Towns (ICT).  However, it did not include direct public 
participation or citizen input.  GMB believes this was a wise approach, as it allowed the County to 
visualize the proposed technological solution(s) and then select a management-governance 
program to implement the Plan recommendations. During execution of the Master plan, public 
participation will be an important process.    
 
1.2  Purpose Statement 
The objective of the Wicomico County – Water and Sewer Master Plan (WC-WSMP) is to outline 
a technical, managerial, and financial approach/policy for adoption by Wicomico County to 
provide safe drinking water and clean water discharge for all residents of Wicomico County in the 
form of a Master Plan document.  No less important is the safeguarding of public health, protection 
against degradation of our water environment, and the enrichment of the economic well-being for 
the Citizens of Wicomico County.  
 
The effort entails evaluating safe drinking water and sewer service capacities and capabilities of 
public (municipalities) and private utility providers, as well as individual onsite systems within 
Wicomico County and document both the available capacities as well as capacity deficiencies, 
leading toward practical and sustainable solutions.   
 
The long-term goal is to have viable safe drinking water and clean water (wastewater) utility 
system(s) serving all residents of Wicomico County, while the short-term goal is to identify and

WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
1-4 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
1.3  Overview and Background  
This overview and background offer a central narrative relative to water and sewer issues from the 
past and how the County might effectively proceed into the future.    
 
The County is faced with many issues applicable to safe drinking water and environmentally 
protective methods of wastewater treatment and disposal.   For example, nine (9) years ago 
Wicomico County was confronted with a serious groundwater contamination issue and was asked 
to assist the Health Department, MDE, and EPA to address a “superfund” groundwater 
contamination event attributed to the toxic chemical trichloroethylene (TCE).  This contamination 
impacted approximately 300 residences in the Morris Mill area of the County.  It precipitated a 
crisis resulting in the inability to sell homes and effectively reducing property values.  The problem 
was addressed directly and resolved by the County with assistance from the two nearby 
municipalities of Fruitland and Salisbury and financial backing from the State of Maryland and 
the Rural Development program under the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA).  The 
installation of a central water distribution system, including an elevated water storage tank for fire 
protection, was accomplished in collaboration with the City of Fruitland water system.  While the 
Morris Mill water system project was underway, the County was alerted that contaminated 
drinking water discovered in another residential subdivision, attributable to trace amounts of a 
discontinued agricultural herbicide.  This problem was cured with individual onsite water 
treatment units, with the expectation the units would be temporary and not permanent.  
 
Failing OSDS have been a concern within the County for many, many years.  In the past, from the 
1960’s through the 1990’s, the solution was central sewer and water systems, and these systems 
were constructed in Fruitland, Sharptown, Pittsville, Hebron, and Willards.  They were constructed 
under considerable pressure from the State of Maryland and with major financial assistance from 
the State of Maryland and the EPA.  The County established the Urban Services Commission in 
the 1960’s which was used primarily to provide service to residential areas contiguous to the City 
of Salisbury and the Town of Delmar.  During this time, the Health Department regulations for 
OSDS were more flexible and permissive (less environmentally protective), and failing systems 
were often allowed to continue in operation using groundwater penetration as a remedy.  The 
magnitude and scope of failing septic systems in the Metro Core area were identified in a 1979 
D R A F T

<!-- page 27 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
 
1-5 
Feasibility Study.(16) Most of the subdivisions identified with failing septic systems identified back 
then have not yet been connected to central sewers (e.g., Chesapeake Heights, Maple Plains, Route 
346 corridor).     
 
Starting in the 1980’s and continuing today with the Chesapeake Bay Program, the onsite disposal 
system regulations by MDE have become more restrictive, inflexible, and protective of 
groundwater and, in our opinion, these restrictions are supported and justified by science.    
However, current onsite disposal system regulations are difficult for the Health Department to 
administer in a way that allows remedies that are practical and affordable.  This stems mostly from 
high groundwater tables and areas where the soils drain very slowly.  Therefore, failing onsite 
disposal systems without practical remedies have severely hindered real estate transactions in the 
County.  This has a significant detrimental economic impact by reducing property values and, in 
turn, reducing County tax revenues.  In some cases, houses are being abandoned for lack of an 
affordable solution for fixing failing onsite disposal systems.  
  
In undertaking the Water and Sewer Master Plan, the first inclination might be to assume the 
County is responding only to individual citizens with failing septic systems and/or contaminated 
drinking water supply wells.  That limited point of view is not accurate.  The Water and Sewer 
Master Plan takes a more all-inclusive approach that is beneficial to all County residences both 
inside and outside municipal corporate boundaries.  Furthermore, its intent is for the County to 
develop a proactive and collaborative approach to further improve the quality of life by facilitating 
affordable and sustainable water and sewer utility service both inside and outside municipal 
corporate boundaries.  Another major factor compelling the County to undertake the Master Plan 
is the multiple programs and policies at the State and Federal level requiring the County to address 
all forms of pollution including nutrient discharges, polluted stormwater runoff, and agricultural 
impacts.  These programs often have acronyms such as TMDLs, WIPs, MS4s, BRNR as well as 
programs for “smart growth” and Tier mapping.  The challenge facing the County is achieving 
compliance with pollution prevention initiative in a way that respects and hopefully collaborates 
where mutually beneficial with each municipalities’ Comprehensive Plan and growth priority.   
The Chesapeake Bay Program, under the auspices of EPA and the associated Bay Restoration Fund 
D R A F T

<!-- page 28 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
1-6 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
established by the State of Maryland, will be significant stakeholders and major drivers for funding 
water and sewer infrastructure in Wicomico County.    
 
Currently, all water and sewer infrastructure built by the County over the past 50 years has been 
accomplished under the authority of the Urban Service Commission, which is provided for in 
Chapter 97 of the Wicomico County Code, Urban Service Districts (USD).  These central water 
and sewer systems constructed by the County are connected to municipal water and systems which 
provide either the water supply or wastewater treatment or both.  Furthermore, the municipalities 
provide all operation and maintenance functions for this infrastructure.   Accordingly, the County 
does not directly have personnel, resources, or a defined department to provide water and sewer 
utility service to County residents.  However, this Master Plan has identified how the County 
indirectly does have a small subset of personnel and capabilities to build and operate small scale 
water and sewerage systems.  The County provides funding to the Wicomico County Board of 
Education (WCBOE) which has in-house capabilities to build and operate commercial scale water 
and wastewater systems to serve County schools.   As an example, the WCBOE operates a 
wastewater treatment plant with a capacity of 14,000 gpd to serve Mardela High School and 
Northwestern Elementary School.  This treatment plant was upgraded for nutrient reductions and 
has a permit for a surface water discharge to Barren Creek.  In this context, the County might be 
considered in the “water and sewer business.”  It should come as no surprise that providing water 
and sewer utility services is not part of the core mission of the WCBOE, and hopefully this Master 
Plan will provide a pathway for the County to assume this role for mutual benefit to all citizens. 
Water and sewer as a “utility service” is essential for the quality of life we expect.  As a utility 
service, it can be argued to be just as importance as electric services and more important than 
telephone service or cable TV service.  It is not uncommon for households to annually pay between 
$1,000 to $3,000 per year for telephone and cable TV service.  Therefore, citizens should have 
forbearance for water and sewer utility bills that are most often less expensive by comparison.   
 
In the past two decades, municipal Comprehensive Plans approved by the State of Maryland have 
taken the position that central water and sewer service will only be extended to residences willing 
to be annexed into their corporate limits.  The consequence of these actions restricts the County, 
in most places, from using a USD for County residences contiguous to municipal boundaries.   This 
D R A F T

<!-- page 29 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
 
1-7 
is not to say that USDs are obsolete or cannot be implemented.  What it means is that for future 
USDs, the County will need to provide not only the water distribution piping and sewer collection 
piping, but also the water supply wells and water/wastewater treatment facilities.   Additionally, 
the County will have to operate, maintain, and manage these utility systems.   This translates into 
the County government providing full water and sewer utility services to selected County residents 
within USDs under an enterprise funding scenario.   In essence, the County will be in the water 
and sewer “utility business” and will need to hire additional County staff for operations, 
maintenance, meter reading, billing and collections, monitoring and reporting, and overall 
management.  Acknowledging the need for the County to get into the water and sewer utility 
business, the Master Plan investigates alternative governing and management entities to best serve 
that purpose.  In other words, the Master Plan will outline the pros and cons of the County operating 
within the existing Chapter 97 of the County Code for Urban Services or alternatively, creating a 
separate water and sewer department within County government or establishing a water “Authority 
or District” to operate semi-autonomously from the County Council.   
  
With this Master Plan, the County is not looking to restrict growth and expansion of any 
municipality.  It is not looking to take over the water and sewer utility systems of any municipality.   
To the contrary, the County is open to mutual collaboration and exploring synergies where County 
resources combined with municipal utilities can enable and implement solutions to solve public 
health problems, and satisfy increasing stringent environmental regulations at the most affordable 
and sustainable cost to the users of these services. The Master Plan provides information and 
guidance for the County to develop the in-house capability to provide water and sewer utility 
services, and thereby bring centralized water and sewer solutions to solve problems that cannot be 
solved on an individual level with replacement or upgrading failing septic systems or wells in 
contaminated groundwater aquifers.   
 
Per the Wicomico County Comprehensive Plan,(1) in 2017 there were approximately 18,000 
housing units connected to municipal water and sewer systems, and a roughly equal number of 
housing units served by individual onsite water and septic systems.  This equates to an existing 
total of approximately 36,000 households in Wicomico County.  The Water and Sewer Master 
Plan has projected household growth to the full build-out capacity of the County based upon the 
D R A F T

<!-- page 30 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
1-8 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
established Zoning and Land-Use plans for the County and all of its municipalities (Reference 
Figure 1-0, 2017 Comprehensive Plan Use).  This total number of households is estimated to be 
approximately 50,000 households by the year 2040.  This Master Plan projected the full build-out 
potential for housing units in the County, as the water and sewer infrastructure proposed herein 
would be expected to have a useful life of more than 50 years.  The purpose of this exercise is to 
give an upper limit to the number of housing units requiring individual safe drinking water wells, 
and project a total pollutant load remaining after treatment for recycling or returning safely back 
into the environment.  When implementing central water and sewer utility services, the capital 
investment cost is very high, and the buried infrastructure is expected to have a useful life 
exceeding 50 years and often extending to 100 years.   
 
Of the 18,000 County households, approximately 10,000 households appear to be candidates for 
connection to municipalities.  Approximately 1,000 households are in unincorporated villages, and 
approximately 3,000 to 4,000 households in subdivision communities are candidates for 
independent or satellite sewer systems presumably built and operated by the County.  Some of 
these households may remain with their individual wells while others may be served by a central 
or community water system.  The remaining existing households within the County, approximately 
5,000, are in rural low-density areas, and identified as limited for residential growth and zoned 
only for agriculture and low-density housing.  For these existing households as well as for all future 
growth in theses zoned areas, this Plan proposes that individual wells and OSDS will be the long-
term permanent solution and that centralized water and sewer service will not be built in these 
areas.  These zoned areas are also identified by the State of Maryland Department of Planning to 
have only limited residential growth under various Smart Growth and Tiered Development 
initiatives.  These areas are also protected from development by their designation as Critical Areas, 
Forest Conservation areas, flood plain management areas, wetlands, and resource conservation 
areas.  The result is that for any recurring individual failures of onsite systems in these areas, the 
affected households will need to look to the Health Department of Wicomico County and the State 
of Maryland, rather than the Wicomico County for governmental assistance.  We have designated 
these areas and households as being within “Regulated Onsite Areas.”  
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 31 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
 
1-9 
Certain areas within Wicomico County have been described in the County Comprehensive Plan of 
2017 as Rural Villages.  These areas are unincorporated and lack central water and sewer systems.  
Most are distinguishable as having their own Post Office.  For instance, six (6) of the eight (8) 
Rural Villages listed below have their own post office.  The two (2) exceptions are Powellville and 
Whitehaven.  
 
▪ Allen  
▪ Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin 
▪ Parsonsburg 
▪ Powellville  
▪ Quantico  
▪ Whitehaven 
 
These URCs have been around for many years, meaning that their individual water supply wells 
and septic systems date back more than 50 years ago, and the individual lots sizes are too small to 
comply with the current minimum lot sizes for onsite septic systems.  Typically, OSDS were not 
expected to last more than 30 to 40 years.  They were expected to eventually be replaced by central 
water and sewer systems.  Newer technologies approved by the State of Maryland provide 
advanced treatment levels and could be expected to provide an onsite solution indefinitely into the 
future.  This Master Plan proposes that when system failures in URC areas cause public health or 
environmental pollution which cannot be managed by the Health Department for the State of 
Maryland, that the County be prepared to implement a central water and/or sewer system and 
connect these residences to a County owned and operated water or wastewater treatment system 
as appropriate.  Generally, these systems will serve the existing Rural Villages with treatment 
plants sized in the range of 20,000 gpd to 40,000 gpd and which, to the maximum practical extent, 
would be uniform in design, configuration, and operations lending itself to more cost-effective and 
efficient operation by a common County staff and management plan.  There are a few exceptions; 
namely, the Rural Villages of Bivalve-Nanticoke –Tyaskin whereby a joint regional treatment 
system is envisioned starting with a capacity of 150,000 gpd per day and potentially expanding if 
planned growth materializes.   
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 32 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
1-10 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
In addition to Rural Villages, this Master Plan has identified numerous subdivision developments 
with “failing” OSDS.  The term “failing” encompasses many causes, but a few of the primary 
causes would be aging systems, clogged discharge piping, and high groundwater levels.   For 
subdivisions experiencing high OSDS failure rates, this Master Plan, in certain instances, will 
recommend central sewerage collection with treatment and disposal at a County wastewater 
treatment plant.  These subdivisions will be identified and grouped into a Community Service 
District.   This Plan will also identify and categorize subdivisions not currently experiencing high 
OSDS failure rates, as Community Service Districts even though installation of central sewerage 
systems may be many years into the future.  Chapters 2 and 3 of the Master Plan will describe the 
Community Service Districts in more detail.   
 
There are approximately 9,000 existing households within proximity to incorporated Cities and 
Towns (ICTs) where the most practical technical solution is connection to existing infrastructure.   
In some cases, the ICTs have included unsewered County residences within the municipal growth 
element of their Comprehensive Plans.   In these areas, treatment capacity is available or planned.    
In other cases, the ICTs do not have the necessary capacity, and system expansions will be 
necessary.   In most cases, the requirement for annexation in conjunction with connection will be 
mandatory and non-negotiable.   The annexation provision will vary among ICTs and will need to 
be addressed individually.  Annexations have political implications which are beyond the scope of 
this Master Plan.    However, within the scope of the Master Plan, the County, in conjunction with 
George, Miles and Buhr (GMB), proactively met with representatives of the ICTs and their 
engineers.  These meetings encompassed open discussions regarding the problems, opportunities, 
and constraints for collaboration or partnerships for effective mutually beneficial alliances that 
would provide safe drinking water and protect the environment at the most affordable and 
sustainable cost to the citizens of Wicomico County.  The minutes from these discussions are 
included in Appendix A.  As regulations become increasingly more advanced and stringent and 
mandated by State or Federal regulations to protect the environment and counteract pollution, it 
may be too much of a financial burden for smaller municipalities.  Mutually beneficial alliances 
that take advantage of economies of scale and the sharing of human resources are explored in this 
Master Plan for the County’s consideration and implementation.    
 
D R A F T

WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
 
1-11 
1.4  Description of the Plan 
The Wicomico County Water & Sewer Master Plan (WC-WSMP) was developed to inform and 
guide Wicomico County with planning and implementing appropriate infrastructure solutions for 
the residents experiencing problems with individual wells and/or failing OSDS.   GMB has been 
tasked to gather the information and report the options to the County to provide solutions.    
 
Safe drinking water and environmentally safe sewage disposal systems are fundamental to public 
health and environmental protection.  Safe and reliable drinking water is essential for life and 
public health.   Proper sewage handling and disposal is essential for public health and necessary to 
protect against environmental degradation.   To provide public water and sewer utility service that 
is practical, cost-effective, and sustainable is the mission of the Master Plan.    
 
The Master Plan outlines a technical, managerial, and financial approach for the County to adopt 
and implement now and carry forward into the foreseeable future as growth and development 
occurs.   The recommended solutions contained herein should serve as a strong guideline for 
preparation of the next Wicomico County Water and Sewerage Plan required by State law and 
COMAR. 
 
The Master Plan takes into consideration the existing water and sewer infrastructure owned and 
operated by the seven (7) incorporated ICTs, Wicomico County Board of Education (WCBOE) 
facilities, private utility systems, and individual onsite water and septic disposal systems.  
Additionally, the Master Plan takes into consideration the role of the County, the ICTs, and the 
Wicomico County Health Department, (WCHD) in compliance with State and Federal regulations, 
to formulate a vision for the most cost-effective and practical provision for water and sewer utility 
service to the citizens of Wicomico County.    
 
The Master Plan is a planning document with recommendations for the County to enact legislature, 
undertake capital infrastructure projects, and/or enter into inter-governmental agreements.  It 
considered the creation a new, separate water and sewer utility department within County 
Government, or in the alternative, creation of a not-for-profit Water Authority. Both of which 
would expand upon the limited capability of the County’s Urban Services Commission.    
D R A F T

<!-- page 34 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
1-12 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
 
This Master Plan demonstrates that Wicomico County is proactively considering the water and 
sewer needs of residents not connected to existing municipal water and wastewater utility systems.  
This plan explores partnerships (service agreements) with existing municipalities.  This plan 
explores creation of County sponsored service areas and utility infrastructure systems.   
 
Wicomico County is looking for the Master Plan to identify the problems plaguing existing water 
and sewer systems within the County and develop a plan to not only address existing deficiencies, 
but also proactively, implement a technical, managerial, and financial solution.   Such a solution 
may involve the County creating another department within county government, such as, the 
County Water & Sewer Department, whose purpose is to plan, design, build, operate, maintain, 
financially manage, and expand water and/or sewer utility systems to County residents, not served 
by municipalities.      
 
This Master Plan will look 50 plus years and beyond, to project population and housing growth in 
accordance with currently established zoning categories, land-use plan, and comprehensive plan.   
It will attempt to crystal ball the County’s water and sewage needs as County approaches its full 
build-out capacity.       
 
It is important to recognize that Wicomico County is the leading agricultural producer in the State 
and that a large percentage of the County’s acreage is zoned and intended for low density 
residential.     Where residential density is low requiring 2 or more acres per residence, the Master 
Plan will recommend that these areas be designated as “Regulated Onsite Areas” whereby 
Wicomico County Health Department under the Maryland Department of the Environment, will 
take on the responsibility of being the governmental unit of first responsibly to regulate and 
manage onsite water and sewer systems.    
 
1.5  Use of Terms/Acronyms  
There are numerous terms and acronyms associated with water and sewer infrastructure and 
associated government programs.    Some of these terms and acronyms are described below to help 
the reader get familiar with the “jargon” or “lingo.” 
D R A F T

<!-- page 35 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 1: Master Plan Introduction 
 
1-13 
 
▪ Drinking Water is a term associated with potable water and meaning safe for drinking. 
 
▪ Clean Water is a term associated with end-product of a wastewater treatment plant, 
sometimes referred to as effluent.   Water is classified as “clean” when it meets the legal 
requirements under a surface water or groundwater discharge permit.  Treated wastewater 
is the same as clean water.  
 
▪ Water Authority is the term being used in this Master Plan to describe an agency, 
commission, or utility provider created by government legislature to provide either 
drinking water or clean water or stormwater services.  Examples of a Water Authority are 
the Somerset County Sanitary District, Inc.; the Dorchester County Sanitary District; the 
Washington Suburban Sanitary Commission (WSSC); and the Easton Utilities. 
 
▪  Sewerage System is a general term inclusive of collection, pump stations, transmission, 
treatment, and disposal of municipal wastewater.   
 
▪ Wastewater Collection System refers only to the central sewage collection without 
inclusion of treatment or disposal.  
 
▪ WIP stands for Watershed Implementation Plan and is a document that Wicomico is 
required to prepare and implement by State and Federal law.  
 
▪ TMDL stands for Total Maximum Daily Loads and refers to a scientific document created 
by the State of Maryland under EPA law to limit pollution to waters in Wicomico County 
that do not meet the State Water Quality Standards. 
 
▪ NPDES stands for National Pollutant Discharge Elimination System is the official legal 
permit allowing for clean water or treated wastewater effluent to discharge to a surface 
water body.

▪ ICTs is an acronym created for this Master Plan and refers to an incorporated municipal 
city or town in Wicomico County. 
 
▪ URCs is an acronym created for this Master Plan and refers to an unincorporated rural 
village or area, such as Quantico, Whitehaven, or Parsonsburg, as well as subdivision 
Communities such as Deer Harbour, Nutters Crossing,  Rustic Acres, and Centennial.  
 
▪ USD stands for Urban Service District created by Wicomico County under Code 97 and 
the Urban Service Commission  
 
▪ OSDS stands for onsite sewage disposal system and refers to individual household septic 
tank or treatment unit along with underground discharge by a drain field or sand mound.

Chapter 2: Existing Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
2-1 
Chapter 2:  Existing Water and Sewer Infrastructure  
 
2.1  General 
Wicomico County has a current population of about 110,000 citizens occupying about 40,000 
residences.  Safe drinking water and clean wastewater utility systems are almost exclusively 
provided by seven (7) of the eight (8) municipalities in Wicomico County.  The existing 
municipalities currently serve about 19,500 residents as documented in the Wicomico County 
Comprehensive Plan(1).  The other 20,000 residents in the County are served by private onsite 
systems or private community systems.   
 
Water consumption in Wicomico County is approximately 28 million gallons per day (mgd).   Of 
that amount, residential and commercial demand is approximately 15 mgd and the remaining 13 
mgd is consumed by agriculture.  
 
Currently, Wicomico County through the Urban Services Commission typically addresses water 
and sewer issues by developing an agreement with municipalities to provide water and accept 
sewage within their municipal infrastructure systems.  The County is involved in helping finance 
and construct the water distribution system and/or sewage collection system, but when completed, 
the municipality takes over the operation and maintenance and billing for these services.   This 
approach has worked reasonably well for about 40 years from the 1960’s through the 1990’s.   
Recently, the municipalities have developed Comprehensive Plans and Amended City Code to 
require annexation as a pre-condition to providing water and sewer, and this pre-condition has 
become a major impediment to further use of this approach.    
 
The current county population supporting 40,000 residences should eventually grow to 60,000 
households and ultimately approach 80,000 residences when the population doubles.    
 
This Chapter of the Master Plan will describe the current water and sewer infrastructure as it exists 
today throughout the County.  It will begin within the western side of the County starting with 
Sharptown and move eastward, ending with Powellville.  It will cover all political jurisdictions 
within Wicomico County starting with County Government and including the eight (8) 
D R A F T

<!-- page 41 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
2-2 
 Chapter 2: Existing Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
incorporated cities and towns (ICTs) listed as follows.  Refer to Figure 2-0 for a key map of the 
existing Municipal ICT areas.   
 
▪ Town of Sharptown 
▪ Town of Mardela Springs 
▪ Town of Hebron 
▪ Town of Delmar, MD 
▪ City of Salisbury 
▪ City of Fruitland 
▪ Town of Pittsville  
▪ Town of Willards 
 
For this Master Plan, the term ICTs will be used to collectively refer to the eight (8) incorporated 
cities and towns listed above.   Except for Mardela Springs, what distinguishes the ICTs from other 
county households, outside of ICTs, is that they provide centralized municipal full-service water 
and sewerage systems, complete with water supply wells, water treatment and disinfection units, 
water storage tanks for fire protection, and distribution systems.  On the sewerage side, the 
centralized infrastructure includes gravity collection sewers, pump stations, pressure force mains, 
a wastewater treatment facility with a State/Federal Permit for discharge to a receiving stream or 
river.   The last component of the central sewerage system is the outfall pipeline from the treatment 
plant to the receiving stream or river.   
 
Figures 2-1 to 2-8 were based on growth tier maps and septic law implementation plans as 
recorded by the Maryland Department of Planning.  Under the Sustainable Growth & Agricultural 
Preservation Act of 2012 (Senate Bill 236), local jurisdiction worked with Planning representatives 
to develop a map showing the implementation of their adopted growth tiers.  Mapping the Growth 
Tiers was based on existing local government plans and goals for growth and land preservation. 
Typically Tier mapping was a reflection of existing zoning, comprehensive plans, and sewer 
service.  It should be noted this mapping was geared toward future residential development areas.  
Local municipalities do have the capability of amending tier maps through an application and legal 
review process.  For the purpose of this Master Plan, these maps were reviewed to have a better 
D R A F T

<!-- page 42 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 2: Existing Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
2-3 
understanding of each ICTs development plans and areas intended to provide future municipal 
sewer service.  The following Tier categories were mapped: 
 
▪ Tier I areas are currently served by public sewerage systems. 
▪ Tier II areas are planned to be served by public sewerage systems. 
▪ Tier III areas are not planned to be served by sewerage systems. These are areas where 
growth by onsite septic systems can occur. 
▪ Tier IV areas are planned for preservation and conservation and prohibit residential 
major subdivisions. 
 
The primary focus of the Master Plan is investigating and understanding the water and sewer 
infrastructure used by individual housing units outside of an ICT, but within the County.  The plan 
will provide advice, suggestions, and recommendations (a plan forward) to the County for dealing 
with system failures that are becoming increasingly more problematic, difficult, and expensive to 
repair or replace.   It is important to recognize that any failures of individual onsite wells and septic 
systems create potential public health concerns and environmental degradation issues.  When all 
onsite systems were originally installed years ago, they were allowed and permitted under the rules 
and regulations of the County Health and the State of Maryland Health Department.  The 
Wicomico County Government was not directly involved in the permitting, operation, and 
maintenance of individual water and sewer infrastructure.  As housing densities increased in the 
ICTs and failing onsite systems increased, central water and sewer infrastructure was installed, 
often at the urging or direct order from the State of Maryland or the Federal Government through 
the EPA.   In all cases, we think it is safe to say that a major source of funding these systems came 
from “grant” money from State and/or Federal sources.  In the 1970’s through the 1980’s, sewerage 
systems could receive up to 87.5% grant dollars of the eligible costs.   
 
This Chapter of the Master Plan looked at the general status and conditions of onsite well and 
septic systems in the County in three categories:  
 
▪ Unincorporated Rural Villages (URVs)  
D R A F T

WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN

 Chapter 2: Existing Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
▪ Households in proximity to ICTs either within the municipal growth boundaries as 
described in each ICTs Comprehensive Plan or the Town Transition areas of the County 
Comprehensive Plan. 
▪ Households in the rural areas of the County generally zoned as rural agricultural and 
designated as agricultural resource land use in the County Comprehensive Plan.    
 
The term URVs was developed to distinguish named communities with the County apart from the 
ICTs.  These URVs are often the communities immediately surrounding US Post Office locations 
and generally have older residences built on “small” lots.   In this context, “small” lots refers to 
the acreage of these lots not being large enough to comply with current onsite septic systems 
regulations.  The other category of URVs are older home communities (more than 40 years) with 
aging and problematic onsite well and septic systems.  Examples of these communities would be 
Chesapeake Heights, Maple Plans, Jersey Road, and the East Main Street corridor east of Civic 
Avenue.  Refer to Figure 2-0 for a key map of the URV service areas.  A listing of eight URVs is 
shown below:  
 
▪ Nanticoke 
▪ Bivalve 
▪ Tyaskin 
▪ Whitehaven 
▪ Quantico 
▪ Allen 
▪ Parsonsburg 
▪ Powellville 
 
The Land Use Plan for a vast portion of Wicomico County is designated as “agricultural resource 
area” and is shown on Map 7-2 of the County Comprehensive Plan and Figure 1-0 of this Master 
Plan.  This Water & Sewer Master Plan is specifically NOT INTENDED to enable centralized 
water and sewer throughout Wicomico County.  Wicomico is the leading “agricultural” County in 
the State of Maryland.  Agriculture has the highest economic impact within the County, and 
nothing in this Water & Sewer Master Plan is intended to diminish or be detrimental to its success.   
One of the important outcomes of this Plan is to make it clear that Wicomico County does not

Some of the ICTs have existing Urban Service Areas or “Out of Town” customers.  These areas

2.2.1 Town of Sharptown  
The Town of Sharptown is an incorporated community with approximately 290 existing residential 
units.  The Town has a public water and sewer system.  The water system consists of multiple 
wells treated for corrosion control (when needed) and chlorine disinfection prior to discharge into 
the community storage tank and distribution system.  Current water demands are approximately 
0.08 MGD.  The sewer system consists of a collection system to a secondary Activated Sludge 
wastewater treatment plant (WWTP).  The WWTP has a permitted capacity of 0.15 MGD.  
Wastewater flows average 0.12 MGD which include inflow and infiltration within the sewer 
collection system.   
 
Inflow and infiltration (I&I) are a result of rainwater and groundwater that enter the sewer pipes 
through cracks, leaky pipe joints, connection failures and deteriorated manhole covers.  I&I 
increases a municipalities total wastewater flow as compared to the actual community demand, 
which is more representative of the water flows.     
 
The County and GMB met with officials of the Town of Sharptown, along with their engineering 
consultant Davis Bowen & Friedel, Inc (DBF) and State Planner Ms. Tracey Gordy.    It was an 
open exchange of information about water and sewer infrastructure owned and operated by 
D R A F T

Sharptown and its current condition and capacities and future needs for upgrading and expansion 
to accommodate infill growth within Town boundaries or growth through annexation.   
 
During the meeting several key items were noted:    
 
1. Town of Sharptown has worked in detail with Maryland Department of Planning (MDP) 
to develop Growth Tier maps and review future sewer and water service areas.  These maps 
have been adopted by the Town.  County and GMB should review adopted Growth Tier 
maps and be mindful of Tier 4 and other designated non-growth areas.   
 
2. Sharptown policy is to require annexation for any properties which they provide water and 
sewer.   
 
3. Discussed the Sam Domingo community as an example project.  If existing septic systems 
were to fail in this area, the County could partner with Sharptown to provide public sewer 
or water to this community.  This may include County funding infrastructure upgrades 
while Sharptown develops Urban Service to provide the public sewer or water.  The County 
could also consider installing a package sewer or water system in this area.  The goal of all 
future projects would be to provide a win-win situation for all involved, while providing 
the necessary water/sewer utilities to maintain public health.   
 
4. Existing Town of Sharptown water and sewer infrastructure capacity were reviewed. 
a. Wastewater: Activated Sludge plant rated at 150,000 GPD.  
b. Water: 
i. Four (4) existing wells.  Two (2) deep wells and two (2) shallow wells.  
Blend three (3) at a time.  
ii. Elevated storage capacity of 200,000 gallons.   
iii. Water system only required disinfection.   
 
5. Concerns were expressed by the Town regarding the Master Plan and requirements or 
financial burdens this may place on the Town.   County noted the goal is for this to be a

2.2.2 Town of Mardela Springs  
The Town of Mardela Springs is an incorporated community with approximately 320 existing 
residential units.  Mardela Springs currently does not provide municipal water and sewer services 
within their incorporated boundary.  Planning studies have been completed in the past to review 
alternatives and cost associated with the Town developing a municipal water and sewer system.    
Current records show concerns of contamination in existing wells due to failing septic systems.  In 
addition, the Wicomico Health Department has record of failing septic systems in the Mardela 
Springs area.   
 
The County and GMB met with officials of the Town of Mardela Springs.     It was an open 
exchange of information about the County goals and purpose statement associated with the Master 
Plan.  The possibility of a partnership between the County and the Town to install public sewer 
and water infrastructure within t and around the Town municipal boundaries was reviewed.  GMB 
noted the full area served may extend beyond the Town’s existing corporate boundaries in order 
to include Mardela Springs schools and other neighboring properties.  The Town would still govern 
and manage any future developments within the corporate boundaries under the existing planning 
and zoning requirements.    
 
 During the meeting several key items were noted: 
 
1. GMB discussed potential project scenarios that could include a partnership between the 
Town and County.  For example, the County could install public sewer and public water to 
serve the Town and neighboring parcels.  The County would own and maintain the public 
infrastructure and charge residents based on usage in order to maintain the system.  The

2.3  Unincorporated Rural Villages 
Within the County there are eight (8) community village areas that consist of a cluster of residents 
that were developed around US Post Office locations generally over 40 years ago.  As previously 
noted, these villages typically have smaller lots and were never served with public water and sewer.  
The lots have aging well and onsite septic systems which the septic systems are failing or have 
reached the anticipated life of the onsite disposal system.  With the smaller lot sizes, it is difficult 
to find additional land area to replace a failing septic drain field.  In addition, poor draining soils 
and high groundwater tables in these areas do not meet State requirements for onsite disposal 
system and risk future failure due to unsuitable conditions.   
 
Refer to Figure 2-0 for the URV areas located within the County.

 Chapter 2: Existing Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
2.3.1 Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin 
The Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin area consists of a group of three (3) existing village 
communities located along the Nanticoke River.  The three communities span 
approximately 7 miles in total.  The area has approximately 600 existing residential EDUs 
and several commercial businesses.  With proximity to the Nanticoke River, the 
communities are impacted by tidal waters and experience coastal flooding.  The majority 
of the homes are in critical areas, and a number of the existing dwellings have recorded 
failing OSDS due to the high groundwater conditions.  The nutrient loads from failing 
OSDS systems go directly into the Nanticoke River as the area drains into the Nanticoke 
River watershed.  Within the area is Roaring Point Park and Ragged Point Cove which are 
public beach areas managed by Wicomico County.  Due to high bacteria and E. Coli levels, 
these beaches have periodic shutdowns from public access.  The increased levels of 
bacteria and E. Coli are likely related to nearby septic discharges from OSDS.  Central 
sewers would help alleviate some of the concerns with untreated sewage infiltrating into 
the nearby water bodies.   
 
2.3.2 Whitehaven  
The Whitehaven community is the smallest rural village considered in the Master Plan.   It 
has approximately 40 EDUs at present and most are experiencing problems with their 
OSDS.  The land elevation is slightly above sea level, and the community experiences 
street flooding during dry weather conditions with above-normal tidal variations.  All 
homes in the village are in the “critical area,” and the nutrient loading from their OSDS 
have the most direct impact to the Chesapeake Bay.  The community is located directly on 
the Wicomico River and drains into the Lower Wicomico River watershed.  Most of the 
homes are older and a few qualify as “historic.”  Whitehaven needs a community based 
central sewerage collection, treatment, and disposal system as soon as practical.  Potential 
solutions to wastewater issues and a proposed community based central sewerage system 
will be described in Chapter 4.  The dry weather street flooding presents a different set of 
challenges and possible solutions, some of which could directly impact the solution to the 
wastewater problem.  The flooding problems will not be alleviated or exacerbated by a

WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 2: Existing Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
2-23 
central sewerage system.   Solutions to coastal flooding toward achieving coastal resilience 
will require significant input and support at the State and Federal level.    
 
2.3.3 Quantico  
The Quantico community is a cluster of approximately 50 dwellings.  The community 
consists of a number of historic homes on smaller lots less than 1 acre in size.   Quantico 
Creek runs along the south portion of the community, and a portion of the existing 
dwellings are located in critical areas.  Quantico Creek drains into the Nanticoke River 
watershed.  With the smaller lots and older homes, there is a concern of OSDS systems 
failing in this area.   
 
2.3.4 Allen  
The Allen community is located along the border of Wicomico and Somerset County at 
Passerdyke Creek, which drains to Wicomico Creek watershed.  The community includes 
approximately 85 EDUs.  Many homes in the area are older than 50 years and have lot 
sizes approximately 1 acre in size.  The smaller lot sizes do raise concerns with older 
OSDS and minimal area to replace the drain field.  At this time, widespread issues with 
failing OSDS are not recorded.  One of the main residential subdivision in the village, 
Passerdyke Village, was developed in the 1990’s and consists of approximately 35 EDUs.  
The lots in this development are approximately 3/4 acres.  At the time of development, 
more stringent septic regulations were in place, and the OSDS most likely have a reserve 
disposal area suitable for a drain field replacement.   
 
2.3.5 Parsonsburg 
Parsonsburg is located along Old Ocean City Rd. on the east side of the County.  The 
community is considered a Rural Village under the County Comprehensive Plan and 
consists of approximately 130 existing EDUs.   For the purpose of this Master Plan, the 
Parsonsburg area under review has been expanded to include existing residential parcels 
along Old Ocean Rd. from Walston Switch to Parsonsburg Village.  This area along Old 
Ocean City Rd. does have some soils with low permeability rates and, in turn, records have 
shown failures of OSDS in the area since the 1990’s.   The expanded boundary area around

the Parsonsburg community consists of approximately 370 EDUs including the EDUs

near the main intersections of Walston Switch Rd. or Parsonsburg Rd., and these lots do 
struggle with finding land area of replacement disposal fields.  Central sewers would help 
alleviate some of the concerns with failing OSDS in this area.   
 
The existing grade elevation near Parsonsburg is one of the highest in the County.  A higher 
geographic ridge runs north to south in this area, with the west side draining toward the 
Lower Wicomico River watershed, while the east side drains toward the Nassawango 
Creek watershed or Upper Pocomoke River watershed.  The higher elevation of the existing 
grade does provide an advantage to finding land area for sewer disposal that is not impacted 
by high groundwater tables.  More suitable soils would be sandy soils which, based on soil 
maps of the area, do exist in certain pockets and also exist along the higher elevation ridge 
area discussed above.   
 
2.3.6 Powellville  
The Powellville Community consists of approximately 80 existing EDUs including 
residential parcels and a couple commercial properties.  At this time, widespread issues 
with failing OSDS are not recorded in this area.  Lot sizes are larger with the exception of 
a few parcels under 1 acre in size located at the intersection of Mount Hermon Rd. and 
Powellville Rd.  Adkins Mill Pond and Adkins Race stream run along the northern portion 
of the community, but the parcels are not within critical areas.  The Powellville area drains 
into the Upper Pocomoke River Watershed. With the smaller lots and older homes, there 
is still a concern of OSDS systems failing.    
 
2.4  Regulated On-Site Areas 
2.4.1 Onsite Sewer Disposal Systems (OSDS) 
Per the Wicomico County Comprehensive Plan,(1) approximately 20,000 EDUs are on OSDS.  The 
Health Department of Wicomico County and the State of Maryland are the existing governing 
agencies that monitor and provide regulations for OSDS.  The OSDS are located throughout the 
County in all areas not designated as a municipal boundary that provide public water and sewer.  
D R A F T

<!-- page 64 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 2: Existing Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
2-25 
Referencing Figure 1-0, Wicomico County Land Use Plan, the Health Department governs all the 
areas with OSDS including Agricultural Resource areas (white areas), Rural Villages (green areas) 
and higher density areas surrounding municipality boundaries.   
 
The parcels within the Agricultural Resource area typically have larger lots (maximum density of 
1 dwelling per 3 acres).  If an older drain field fails due to age of the system, the land area is 
available in these areas to replace the OSDS.  In addition, if an existing OSDS fails due to site 
conditions, the larger lots provide the area needed to consider alternative solutions.  Advanced 
onsite treatment systems can be installed to treat effluent septic to higher quality.  In turn, 
alternative disposal systems such as drip disposal can be considered with higher quality septic 
effluent.  Larger disposal fields and/or mounded systems can also be installed to correspond with 
the site groundwater and soil properties such as lower application rates of the treated effluent into 
the ground.  Where more land area is available it typically allows alternative solutions to be 
considered for a new OSDS that meets current State Regulation requirements.  One of the goals of 
this Master Plan is to maintain and preserve the rural nature of the Agricultural A-1 land areas and 
maintain consistency with the County’s preservation goals for these rural areas. 
 
Other land areas within the County have increased density including those that surround existing 
municipalities, town transition areas, and areas within rural villages.  Typically, these areas have 
been developed at a higher density compared to those in the Agricultural Resource use.  As 
discussed above, the smaller lots become problematic when the existing OSDS fails, because it is 
difficult to find additional land area to replace a failing septic drain field.  In addition, poor draining 
soils and high groundwater tables in these areas do not meet State requirements for replacement 
with the area available.  
 
The Health Department has recorded multiple areas around the County that have widespread 
recorded failing septic systems.  Most of the areas of failing OSDS are within the higher density 
areas of rural villages or communities with multiple residential units.  A number of County areas 
with high and medium density development are surrounded by existing municipalities.  The 
County worked with the Health Department to develop a map in the 2010 Water & Sewer Plan to 
document these widespread failing OSDS areas.  Refer to Figure 2-9 for the areas that were 
D R A F T

<!-- page 65 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
2-26 
 Chapter 2: Existing Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
defined in 2010.  The County is currently working on an updated map to reflect additional areas 
that also have widespread failing septic systems.  It is becoming more difficult for the Health 
Department and State to develop solutions in these areas when the existing OSDS fails.  Residents 
are unable to sell a home because the OSDS fails during inspections and, in some of the areas 
,residents are abandoning their homes.        
 
2.5  Private Water and Sewer Systems 
Within Wicomico County there are a number of private water and/or sewer systems which serve 
more than one (1) customer but are not part of municipal public system.  These systems are 
typically located in areas outside of a municipal boundary and where public systems were not 
available via an Urban Service or “Out of City Customer” agreement.  Owners of private water 
and sewer systems may include, (1) Residents within a subdivision where the Homeowner’s 
Association may manage the system, (2) Organizations such as the Board of Education,( 3) Private 
or Industrial businesses, or (4) Mobile Home Park property owners.   
 
Private water systems consist of a package water treatment system which will treat the raw well 
water to drinking water standards prior to distribution to its customers.  Similarly, the private sewer 
system consists of a package sewer treatment system designed to treat collected sewage prior to 
disposal to a large community subsurface disposal system.  The cost of maintaining these systems 
and meeting regulations falls on the property owner(s). 
 
Wicomico County regulates the approval of private systems and currently does not allow a package 
system to serve new or expanded residential developments in an A-1 Agricultural district.  As 
discussed above, the County’s goal is to preserve the rural areas for low density residential 
properties (1 dwelling per 3 acres) and agriculture related uses.  Package treatment systems in 
these areas would promote increased growth and density.  Therefore, the County has not recently 
approved new private package systems in A-1 Agricultural areas.   
 
Some private systems do provide more advance treatment levels as compared to OSDS systems 
but some of the private systems are just large community OSDS system.  Private systems can 
become problematic for private Owners to manage and maintain.  Funds need to be reserved to

WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 3: Future Water and Sewer Needs 
 
3-1 
Chapter 3:  Future Water and Sewer Needs 
3.1  General 
The population of Wicomico County has increased by approximately 10,000 citizens every decade 
for the past fifty (50) years.  By 2040 the Wicomico County population could be 130,000 citizens 
as documented in the Wicomico County Comprehensive Plan(1) and may grow to 160,000 citizens 
by 2070.  About 49% of County residents are located within municipalities and are served by 
public water or sewer.  The remaining 51% are on OSDS systems which are failing in many areas 
and contribute high levels of nutrient pollutants and bacteria to our groundwater and the 
Chesapeake Bay.  
 
This Chapter of the Master Plan will estimate future population and the number of households that 
could be built in Wicomico County in conformance with the Comprehensive Plan.  Based on the 
Comprehensive Plan Land Use Map (Figure 1-0), the areas of growth are anticipated to be 
concentrated around the eight (8) existing Incorporated Towns and Cities (ICT) and 
Unincorporated Rural Village (URV) areas.  Current needs to address failing OSDS for existing 
residents were discussed in Chapter 2.  As the population increases in these growth areas, the need 
for public water and/or sewer services will only escalate further.  With 50,000 additional citizens 
and 19,500 new residents by 2070, this will produce 4.875 mgd of increased residential wastewater 
flow.     
 
One reason for reviewing future projections or “full build-out” of the County is to gage the 
potential number of households or “customers” that might need water and/or sewer utility services 
over the next twenty plus years.  This projection will help the County determine which type of 
management structure would be most suitable if they are to provide public water and/or sewer to 
residents.  Furthermore, this future projection will allow the County to adequately plan and size 
the foundation components of utility infrastructure. 
 
3.2  Future Service Areas 
The Wicomico County Land Use Map (Figure 1-0) was analyzed to determine areas of growth 
projections and designate these areas geographically.  All areas, apart from Agricultural Resource 
D R A F T

<!-- page 82 -->
3-2   
 
Chapter 3: Future Water and Sewer Needs 
 
Areas (A-1), were included in future growth projections.   The County’s goal is to preserve the 
rural areas designated as A-1 for low density residential properties and agricultural related uses.  
Therefore, it is not anticipated that parcels in the A-1 area will ever have public water and/or sewer 
service.  All other County areas are being analyzed and future public water, and/or sewer service 
needs are anticipated.   
 
Refer to Figure 3-0: Future Service Area Key Map for existing and proposed areas with public 
water and/or sewer service.  The existing eight (8) ICT areas are designated in blue.  Generally, 
the service area boundary on Figure 3-0 for each ICT is consistent with each municipality’s 
planned growth area or the SB236 Growth Tier maps.  There are some inconsistencies where the 
County designated Town Transition Areas or other Land Use areas that surround a municipal 
boundary, but the municipality has not identified these areas in their own planning maps.  For this 
Master Plan, the ICT boundary will maintain consistency with the 2017 County Land Use Map, 
and therefore the final ICT service area boundary may be larger than that shown on the current 
SB236 Growth Tier maps (Figures 2-1 to 2-8) and incorporate Town Transition areas.  
Modifications to existing planning maps are discussed in more detail in later chapters of this 
Master Plan and maintaining consistency among all planning maps will be a critical action step for 
the County.  
 
The overall goal of this Master Plan is to determine areas that the County should consider providing 
public water and/or sewer infrastructure.  It is anticipated some of the Town Transition areas that 
directly border existing municipal areas will be served by that municipality.  The County’s goal is 
not to negatively impact the growth of existing ICTs but to assist in areas where extension of 
existing public water and/or sewer services are not feasible.   
 
The eight (8) existing URV areas are designated in teal on Figure 3-0.  In addition to the URVs, 
there are several teal areas that are designated as “Unincorporated Communities.”   There are a 
total of five (5) communities designated. 
 
▪ Nanticoke Road Community 
▪ Riverside Road Community 
D R A F T

<!-- page 83 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 3: Future Water and Sewer Needs 
 
3-3 
▪ Delmar East Community 
▪ Coulbourn Mill Road Community 
▪ East Wicomico Community 
 
These communities are areas that have a number of existing residential developments in close 
proximity or multiple clusters of homes with smaller lots.  Some of these areas have been 
designated by the County Health Department to have widespread failing OSDS systems.  While 
these community areas do border some of the existing municipality boundaries, there is no 
knowledge or documentation that the existing municipalities intend to expand into these areas.  In 
addition, the defined community areas are fairly large geographically, and this increases the 
technical and economic feasibility of the County providing public water and/or sewer services to 
these areas.    
 
The eight (8) URVs and five (5) communities are all shown on Figure 3-0 in teal and will now be 
designated as “Unincorporated Rural Villages and Communities” (UVC).   All these designated 
areas are relatively dense and do not currently have access to public water and/or sewer.  It is 
recommended the County focus on providing UVC areas with public water and/or sewer.  In 
addition, Mardela Springs does not have an existing water or sewer system.  It is also recommended 
Wicomico County focus on developing an agreement or partnership to provide Mardela Springs 
with public water and/or sewer.  Refer to Figures 3-1 to 3-19 for detailed maps of each ICT and 
UVC Service Area.   
  
3.3  Future Service Areas Growth Projections 
Parcel data was collected from all service areas to determine the existing EDUs within each service 
area and also develop a projection of the future EDUs for each area.  EDU analysis includes the 
following: 
 
1) Existing:  Existing EDUs plus existing commercial flow within the service area boundary 
developed under this Master Plan.  For existing ICTs, the service area may be larger than 
the existing municipal boundary and include parcels currently within the County but 
anticipated to be served by the municipality in the future. 
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 84 -->
3-4   
 
Chapter 3: Future Water and Sewer Needs 
 
2) Existing + Vacant Lots:  Existing EDUs plus vacant residential lots developed at the New 
Housing Capacity (NHC) as derived from Wicomico County Planning and Zoning data.  
Existing commercial flows are included in these projections.   
 
3) Full Build-Out:  Development of all residential parcels at the maximum NHC designated 
for each parcel plus all commercial flows and future commercial zoned parcels developed. 
 
Provided as Figure 3-20 and Figure 3-21 is a guide to determining existing and future EDU 
projections for both residential and commercial parcels.  Below is Table 3-1: Future Service 
Area EDUs summarizing the service area projections.

Chapter 3: Future Water and Sewer Needs 
 
3-5 
 
Wicomico County should focus on providing public water and/or sewer for the eight (8) URVs 
and five (5) communities.  The existing customer base is approximately 5,200 EDUs.  An estimated 
20-year to 50-year customer base may increase to total of 10,000 EDU customers.  Full Build-out 
of the service areas are not anticipated in the near-term future (50 years), and therefore 
infrastructure should be sized to handle Existing + development of Vacant Lots.   
 
 3.4  Future Regulated OSDS and Private Systems 
As discussed previously and documented in the 2017 County Comprehensive Plan, the County’s 
goal is to maintain and preserve the rural nature of Agricultural Resource Areas.   These areas will 
continue to have onsite wells and onsite OSDS and be regulated by current State of Maryland 
COMAR regulations for onsite systems and per the County Health Department direction.   
 
A number of private water and sewer systems in addition to mobile home parks (MHP) exist 
throughout the County.  At this time, it is not anticipated Wicomico County will take an active 
role in taking over ownership of these private system.  Over time the County may develop 
agreements with private system Owners to assist with maintenance or management.  But the 
County should be cautious of taking responsibility for upgrading existing private systems or taking 
ownership of aged infrastructure that private Owners should be maintaining.   
 
The County may want to take into consideration a different approach with private system that are 
currently owned by the Wicomico County Board of Education (WCBOE).  WCBOE is part of the 
Wicomico County Government and part of the WCBOE budget is incorporated within the County 
budget.  The County and WCBOE may see an advantage and cost savings by combining 
operational or management services associated with water and/or sewer infrastructure.  As the 
County develops public water and/or sewer systems, the management structure and personnel will 
need to be in place to maintain these systems.  The WCBOE does currently employ qualified 
individuals and incur cost to contract Private Operational companies to handle the existing private 
water and sewer infrastructure.  In addition, if the County is serving designated service areas with 
public water and/or sewer systems, and there is a nearby school without public water and/or sewer, 
cost should be evaluated to extend the public services as needed to the school.  This may provide 
D R A F T

<!-- page 86 -->
3-6   
 
Chapter 3: Future Water and Sewer Needs 
 
a long-term savings for both the County and WCBOE as the private systems will need future 
upgrades and maintenance.  As an example, when considering public water and sewer in the Town 
of Mardela Springs, the WCBOE has an existing wastewater treatment plant (WWTP) and stream 
discharge permit at the Mardela Springs schools.   A partnership and/or agreement may allow the 
County to utilize this existing permit by providing more advance treatment to increase the permit’s 
capacity while alleviating any concerns WCBOE may have to future upgrades on the existing 
WWTP.   
 
The existing private systems associated with a MHP are typically owned by only one (1) property 
owner or corporation.  If County owned public water and/or sewer become available in an area 
with an existing MHP private system, then the private owner may choose to connect as a single 
customer.  It will then be the MHP property owner’s responsibility to bill their individual tenants 
or individual mobile homeowners.  The County should not take responsibility for installing the 
infrastructure to serve each mobile home.  It is recommended that internal infrastructure on private 
systems or within private communities should be the responsibility of the private owner.     
 
 
 
D R A F T

Chapter 4:  Water and Sewer Infrastructure    
4.1  Introduction 
Chapter 4 of the Master Plan discusses technological issues affecting water and sewer 
infrastructure.  First, it covers drinking water and then wastewater issues and alternatives.  This 
will lead to recommendations of the preferred technological approaches.    
 
In Chapter 3, the Master Plan provided a listing of nineteen (19) service districts that are proposed 
to meet the future water and/or sewer needs of the County.  Within that list are seven (7) 
incorporated municipalities who already operate and manage water and sewer utility services.  It 
is believed that into the future, these seven (7) municipalities will continue to operate, manage, 
upgrade, and expand their own municipal utility services.    
 
Sharptown 
Hebron 
Delmar 
Salisbury 
Fruitland 
Pittsville  
Willards 
 
As it currently stands, Wicomico County will not participate with these municipal utility systems 
unless conditions change, and the municipalities seek assistance from the County.  On the other 
hand, this Master Plan is recommending that Wicomico County take an active role in the planning 
construction, operations, and management of municipal central community water and sewer 
services in twelve (12) newly defined districts.   
 
These newly defined districts include one (1) incorporated municipality. 
 
Mardela Springs 
 
In addition, eight (8) Rural Villages within the County were divided into six (6) potential water 
and/or sewer service areas. 
 
Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin  
Whitehaven 
Quantico 
Allen
