4.2 Water  
Wicomico County is geologically located within the Atlantic Coastal Plain, which provides an 
abundant supply of groundwater for use as safe drinking water.  This source, barring manmade 
contamination, will continue to be adequate far into the future.  Surface water (as a source for safe 
drinking) such as the Nanticoke River or Wicomico River or streams and ponds, are not 
economically practical for Wicomico County or any of the other Eastern Shore counties.   
 
Portions of the Water Resources Element (Chapter 5) of the 2017 County Comprehensive Plan 
were used as reference, and this information formed the basis for the technological and planning 
issues.  An excerpt from Water Resources Element is restated here:  
 
Groundwater is an important source of drinking water throughout Maryland. 
Most void spaces in rocks beneath the water table contain water, but these 
spaces only become an aquifer when water bearing rock readily carries water 
to wells and springs in significant volumes. On Maryland’s Eastern Shore, 
aquifers are the source of both private wells and larger public water supply 
systems. Unlike the Piedmont and other areas of the Western Shore of 
Maryland, topography and the slow-moving nature of surface streams alleviate 
the need to construct dams for storing and collecting large volume of water 
supplies. In addition, the aquifers under the Eastern Shore have a long history 
of providing high-quality water in substantial volumes.  
Based on the estimates of growth as it relates to the future demand of drinking 
water provided by public water systems and private wells, Wicomico County 
has adequate water supply for its current and future residents. County-wide, the 
total water demand including public and private systems for residential, 
commercial, industrial and agricultural uses is estimated to be 28 MGD. The 
largest consumer being agriculture, which uses approximately 13.294 MGD as 
indicated in…. The majority of the aquifers used for our water supply are 
relatively shallow, compared to other Maryland counties, allowing for reduced 
D R A F T

<!-- page 115 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
 
4-3 
cost of wells. The Coastal Plain aquifer system provides the base flow for 
virtually all of the streams that originate in the Atlantic Coastal Plain. 
Groundwater from this aquifer system also discharges in the Chesapeake Bay. 
 
There are multiple groundwater aquifers available.  Aquifers are divided into two (2) broad 
categories: surficial or confined.  The “surficial” aquifers are exposed to recharge from above 
while the “confined” aquifers are deeper and separated from the surficial aquifers by dense soil 
layers.  
 
The “surficial” aquifer is often referred to as the water table aquifer and with specific names such 
as the Columbia, Salisbury or the Paleochannel aquifers.  It is plentiful and easily obtainable in 
large quantities for the long-term quantity needs of the County.  However, it is susceptible to 
contamination by pollution sources from above such as, nitrogen, bacteria, and chemicals, which 
could render this supply unsafe for drinking without expensive treatment technology.  
 
The “confined” aquifers in the County used for safe drinking water are the Manokin, Pocomoke 
and Fredericka aquifers.  Often, these aquifers have concentrations of iron or magnesium which 
need treatment but remain practical and affordable for producing safe drinking water.   
 
Well head protection studies have been sponsored by the Maryland Geological Survey (MGS), 
MDE and the City of Salisbury within the past 20 years.    
 
The City of Salisbury has continued to proactively invest in additional well treatment and storage 
capacity.  In the past decade, the City has constructed a 2 MG storage tank, made major 
improvements to both the Paleo and Park Water Treatment Plants, and added a third (3) Paleo well 
which will increase the system capacity.  From the Comprehensive Plan of 2010, the City had the 
potential to add 9,530 new housing units inside City limits, plus their designated growth areas.  
The number of new housing units added in the past decade would diminish this total amount.    
 
The current approximate demand upon groundwater, based upon data provided in the County 
Comprehensive Plan is shown in Table 4-1.

Currently, there are approximately 19,000 residences served by municipal water systems and 
roughly an equivalent number using individual wells.  Table 4-1 above demonstrates that principal 
with both sectors using about 5 mgd. The total water consumption for all residences is 
approximately 10 mgd, and this amount generally fits to the engineering standard formula of 100 
gallons per person per day.  In other words, a daily water demand of 10 mgd is representative of a 
population of 100,000 persons.  The approximate number of residential houses using individual 
wells is roughly equivalent to the residential houses connected to public water systems.  The largest 
use of groundwater in the County is for seasonal agricultural irrigation.  Most of 
commercial/industrial water use is supplied by public municipal water systems, rather than 
individual wells.   
 
Assumptions as to the water demands that could be expected 50 to 100 years into the future are 
speculative and could vary significantly.  Land use and zoning decisions will play a factor, and 
these factors could be used to increase or decrease future water demand.  We envision that sea 
level rise currently being experienced, and expected to continue and possibly accelerate, would 
have the effect of reducing water demand.     
 
Future demand as discussed in Chapter 3, based upon full build-out in accordance currently 
promulgated land-use and zoning regulations, suggests that the ultimate residential water demand 
could approach twice what currently exists and result in an additional 10 MG withdrawal from our 
groundwater aquifers.  Based upon studies and reports issued by the Maryland Geological Survey, 
MDE, and the US Geological Survey concerning groundwater quantities and quality, it is 
reasonable to assume that Wicomico County can rely on its groundwater aquifers to support future 
populations consistent with full build-out under current land use and zoning policies.

There is ample quantity (volume) of water in these aquifers to serve these municipalities under all 
scenarios for future growth.  Should the surface aquifers become contaminated with pollutants 
such as nitrates, or toxins such as synthetic organic compounds (SOC), or per- and polyfluoroalkyl 
substances (PFAS), PFAS chemicals options exist for using advanced treatment technologies.    
These technological treatment options could be anion exchange filters, activated carbon filters, or 
advanced oxidation with ozonation.  These advanced treatment options would be compared with 
use of a deeper aquifer to determine the more cost-effective option.  
 
4.2.1 Incorporated Cities and Towns (ICT) 
Table 4.2 below shows the eight (8) municipalities in Wicomico County.  Seven (7) have 
centralized municipal water systems and all use groundwater as the water source.   Mardela Springs 
is the only ICT without a central water system; their municipal residences and commercial 
businesses are served by individual wells.  Table 4-2 identifies the various groundwater aquifers 
being used for the municipal water source.  Some of these aquifers are the shallow surface aquifers; 
the others are confined aquifers.  Fruitland recently installed a new water supply well in the 
Manokin confined aquifer.  This provided redundancy and reliability for the existing wells and 
added protection against surface contamination.   
 
Based upon discussions with the municipalities there was no strong indication that the ICTs would 
favor having the County take over operation of their water utility.  In the absence of a specific 
proposal by the County, and since the County does not currently have a water operations 
department; it would be premature to speculate or make specific recommendations.  The ICTs are 
far enough apart, aside from the metro-corridor of Delmar-Salisbury-Fruitland, that an 
interconnected County sponsored water system would not be technologically desirable or cost-
effective.    
 
Discussion about direct County assistance or support for municipal water systems should be 
delayed into the future, until the County develops operations capacity and experience, or a 
municipality is in extreme financial distress.  The ICTs would still have the option of considering 
a private-for-profit water utility corporation under the Public Service Commission (PSC).

Mardela Springs looked at the feasibility for a central water and/or sewer system in 2011 and 
prepared a Preliminary Engineering Report, with finding from USDA.  The unit cost per residence 
was quite expensive when the system was sized only for Town residents.  However, the unit cost 
per residence was reduced based on a water system sized to serve not only the Town residents, but 
also customers outside Town limits in areas designated as Town transition.  The recommendation 
of this Master Plan is a central water system for the Mardela Springs area sponsored and operated 
by Wicomico County, and the proposed system with an alternate option of entertaining proposals 
from private water utility companies operating under the PSC.

4.2.2 Proposed Community and Villages 
Table 4-3 below shows eleven (11) proposed new service areas comprised of the eight (8) rural 
villages in the County and five (5) community subdivision areas.  For some of these villages and 
communities where future water demand is small, such as Whitehaven or Quantico, the Master 
Plan is suggesting that after installation of a central sewer collection system, there may not be a 
need to install a central water supply system.  This suggestion is based upon several factors, such 
as: 
 
▪ A small customer base that grows very slowly over time might not have enough users to 
make a central water system affordable and sustainable.

▪ An individual home water treatment system is more viable than an individual OSDS, 
because the home water treatment industry is established with many service providers 
compared to OSDS.     
 
▪ Installation of a central sewer system eliminates the public health risk of well 
contamination by OSDS.  
 
▪ The annual cost may be lower than a central water system.  
 
▪ If a future large development project is approved by planning and zoning, under the 
“growth pays for growth” principal, a future growth project could be the catalyst and 
primary funding source for a central water system. 
 
There are other considerations when installing a central water system that would be missed with 
individual wells, such as: 
 
▪ Central water systems can provide hydrants for fire protection. 
 
▪ Houses with fire protection have lower homeowner insurance rates.  
 
▪ Central water systems with elevated storage tanks provide water under pressure during 
power outages.

Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
 
Following are engineering comments and concepts relevant to the villages and communities 
leading to a recommendation for individual wells compared to a central water system provided by 
either the County or a private water utility company.  
 
4.2.3 Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin Villages   
The most pressing need in these three (3) villages is for the installation of a central sewer system 
to protect water quality in the Nanticoke watershed and eliminating the risk of well contamination 
by septic system contamination.  The number of combined homes in these villages should be large 
enough to support a viable central water system.  The area is susceptible to sea level rise and the 
possibility of salt-water intrusion to the water supply aquifers.  From a technological perspective, 
all options are feasible.  The villages could stay with individual wells or they could choose to 
install a public or private central water system.  This Master Plan is recommending as a top choice 
the implementation of a public water system because it better protects against individual well 
contamination, it can facilitate future growth within planning and zoning guidelines, and it appears 
to be economically viable.  As an alternative, this area could consider staying with individual wells 
or entertaining proposals from a private water utility company.   
 
4.2.4 Whitehaven Village 
The most pressing need is installation of a central sewer system because the septic systems are 
failing, and individual properties are subject to dry weather flooding.  The size of the village is too 
small to consider a central water system because the user rates would not be affordable. The village 
is not planned for future growth to an extent that central water system would develop an economy 
of scale to become financially viable.  Therefore, it is proposed that individual water supply wells 
remain as the preferred system for safe drinking water.    
 
4.2.5 Quantico Village 
The number of dwellings in Quantico is about 50.  Individual water supply wells are recommended 
for the future presuming the existing onsite septic disposal systems are replaced with a central 
sewer collection system.  If a significant development project should be planned and approved in 
the future within the Quantico service area, the concept of a central water system or private utility

4-9 
water company could be considered.  In this scenario the future growth development would fund 
the cost of the central water system infrastructure.  The central water system could be turned over 
to the County for operation and maintenance or the central water system could be operated as a 
private water utility under the PSC. 
 
4.2.6 Allen Village 
The existing number of dwellings in the Allen service area are about 90.    The recommendation 
for Allen village area is the same as for Quantico.   Continue the use of individual wells indefinitely 
into the future.       
 
4.2.7 Parsonsburg Village 
The proposed service area for Parsonsburg village and surrounding area extends westward along 
Route 346 to Walston-Switch Road.  The number of houses including existing subdivisions 
suggest that a central water system should be considered.  The lot densities and potential for future 
development, including commercial, in this area further reinforces the consideration of a central 
water system.  Note that the western service area boundary ends at Walston-Switch Road.  The 
area westward on Route 346, when a central water system is requested, is anticipated but not pre-
ordained to be served by the City of Salisbury.  The City of Salisbury water service already extends 
to Beaver Run Elementary School on Route 346 and to Walston-Switch Road along Route 50.  It 
is recommended that the County should begin sooner rather than later, to investigate and evaluate 
a central water system in this Parsonsburg area, and its relationship to the boundary of the City of 
Salisbury water system.   
 
4.2.8 Powellville Village 
The number of dwelling in the Powellville village service area are about 80.  The recommendation 
for Powellville is the same as for Allen and Quantico villages.  Continue the use of individual 
wells indefinitely into the future.

4.2.9 Nanticoke Road Community 
This proposed service area is outside the municipal growth boundary for annexation by the City 
of Salisbury.  The subdivisions and homes are generally less than 40 years old, and the lot sizes 
tend to be at one-half acre or larger.  The individual wells and onsite septic systems were 
constructed under different and more conservative MDE regulations compared to systems 
approved and installed in the village service areas or areas contiguous to the City of Salisbury.  
Currently, there does not appear to be a need to consider a central water system.  With 
consideration of larger lot sizes, better draining soils, and newer OSDS compared to other county 
areas,  it is recommended that individual wells with home treatment systems continue to be utilized 
in the future.   
 
4.2.10 Riverside Road Community 
This community service area consists of several subdivisions south and west of the municipal 
boundary for the City of Fruitland.  A major portion of the existing dwellings are located with the 
Critical Area boundaries.  The need to replace onsite septic disposal systems is more crucial for 
environmental protection and more problematic than concerns over safe drinking water.  The 
spatial distances between subdivisions are of a magnitude that a central water system is not likely 
to be a cost-effective solution.  There are no known toxic contamination issues in this area at the 
present time.  The use of individual wells is recommended for the future. 
 
4.2.11 Delmar East Community  
This service area was created in response to the County Comprehensive Plan land-use designation 
for Town Transition.   Under the Town Transition land use designation, all new development will 
be served by central water and central sewer systems.   From discussion with the Town of Delmar, 
we learned this area is low priority for their future growth and annexation, as the Town’s priority 
is westward growth.  There is insufficient numbers or density of dwellings for the County to 
consider installing a central water or sewer system under the guiding principle of “growth pays for 
growth.”

4-11 
4.2.12 Coulbourne Mill Community 
This service area located “outside” the Route 13 Bypass is east of Fruitland and southeast of 
Salisbury.  Prominent subdivisions in this service area include Deer Harbour, Rustic Acres, 
Foxchase, and Trace Hollow, among others.  The number of existing homes in this proposed 
community service area exceeds 1,100.  It is unlikely that these subdivisions would be annexed 
into the City of Fruitland or the City of Salisbury; partially because these areas are not part of the 
municipal growth plans for Salisbury or Fruitland.  Houses in these areas frequently use water 
softeners and water treatment systems to deal with low pH and high iron concentrations.  The City 
of Fruitland built a 500,000 gallon elevated water storage tank inside the Route 13 Bypass and in 
reasonable proximity to this service area.  The City of Salisbury built a 2 million gallon elevated 
water storage tank near Salisbury University that potentially could help with water supply or 
emergency use.  From a technological perspective, a central water system or continued use of 
individual water supply wells are viable solutions.  Continued use of individual wells and home 
treatment systems will in most cases be affordable.  Homeowners purchased houses in these 
subdivisions with individual wells and treatment systems, and barring groundwater contamination 
by septic disposal systems or toxic chemicals, should remain viable indefinitely.  Should a central 
sewer collection be built in this community, the individual wells will be better protected against 
the risk of septic disposal system contamination.     
 
A central water system does offer some advantages to the Community that are not provided with 
individual wells.  The primary advantages are the ability to continue to supply water under pressure 
in the event of a power outage, the presence of hydrants to provide water for fire protection, as 
well as reducing the cost of homeowners insurance.  Further advantages are the added protection 
of having a professional operations staff monitoring and maintaining safe drinking water.  From a 
technological perspective, either individual wells or a central water system are viable options, 
assuming a central sewer system is installed to reduce potential for groundwater of individual 
wells.   
 
4.2.13 East Wicomico Community 
This service area was created in response to the County Comprehensive Plan land-use designation 
for Town Transition.   Under the Town Transition land use designation, all new development will

be served by central water and central sewer systems.  This potential service area is situated 
between Pittsville and Willards and generally bounded by Route 50 to the south and properties 
bordering Route 346 to the north.  Neither Pittsville nor Willards have identified this area for future 
growth and annexation in their Comprehensive Plans. The Towns could likely provide this service 
area with safe drinking water.  The current number of houses in this service area at around 50 is 
too small and spread out to support a viable central water system.  Under the guiding principle of 
“growth pays for growth,” the County does not need to invest in public infrastructure until such 
time as a significant development project is proposed and ready to contribute a proportionate share 
of the project costs.  
 
4.2.14 Summary of Water Recommendations  
Listed below is a summary of the Master Plan recommendations from a technological perspective 
related to safe drinking water: 
 
A. Develop the resources within County government to implement central safe drinking water 
systems where needed in a proactive and cost-effective manner inclusive of operation, 
maintenance, and management. 
 
B. Proactively assist and/or partner with Mardela Springs to construct a municipal central 
water system to serve not only the Town, but also nearby customers including the 
Wicomico County public schools. 
 
C. Proactively assess the need for a central water system in conjunction with a central sewer 
system for the service area identified as Parsonsburg Village and its relation to the 
boundary of the City of Salisbury water system.  
 
D. Proactively monitor sea-level rise and its impact on individual wells, and assess the need 
for a central water system in the Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin area.

4.3  Wastewater (Clean Water)  
Under EPA and MDE programs, the term Clean Water is used to refer to wastewater that has 
undergone treatment to the level required by an authorized surface water or groundwater discharge 
permit.  The term wastewater more narrowly refers to untreated sewage before it has undergone 
treatment; however, it is generally used interchangeably with Clean Water.  The technical term 
“effluent” is often used and can be synonymous with treated wastewater or Clean Water.   
 
In contrast to the abundance of groundwater as a source of safe drinking, Wicomico County faces 
difficult and challenging obstacles to finding and permitting clean water disposal options.  The 
County is blessed with many waterbodies and watersheds both tidal and non-tidal; all of which 
contribute to the Chesapeake Bay watershed.  The Chesapeake Bay watershed does not have an 
unlimited capacity to receive the amounts of nutrients and pollutants remaining in the discharge 
after treatment.  This is even applicable when using the highest level of wastewater treatment, 
labeled as Enhanced Nutrient Removal (ENR).    
 
Perhaps the biggest challenge facing Wicomico County in terms of providing central sewer 
systems is obtaining a permit from MDE for discharges.  The Nanticoke River, Wicomico River, 
and Pocomoke River watersheds have limited capacity to accept the additional nutrient loadings 
from treatment plants.  This load capacity has been defined by MDE in terms of TMDLs, which 
stands for Total Maximum Daily Loads in conformance with federal law which was enacted in 
1972 by the Clean Water Act.  TMDLs are a tool used by MDE to manage and maintain water 
quality standards established for the receiving water body.  TMDLs are the sum of the allowed 
pollutant loads for point source, nonpoint source, projected growth, and a margin of safety.    
 
TMDL = Point Sources + Nonpoint Sources + Projected Growth + Margin of Safety 
 
The process of applying for a surface water discharge permit for a municipal wastewater discharge 
may be expected to take 12 to 18 months and require a significant expense in providing necessary 
supporting documentation.  The process involves a lengthy review process, public notice, and 
public participation.  There are no assurances that MDE will ultimately approve or grant a 
discharge permit.  The seven ICTs in the County with municipal wastewater treatment plants have 
surface water discharge permits from MDE for their current design capacity, but are not assured

the loading limits in the existing permit for discharge to Burnt Mill Branch limits growth to the 
current design flow capacity.  An alternate land-based discharge or the purchase of nutrient credits 
will be needed to support future growth.

One of the more important action items from the Master Plan is for the County to proactively seek 
acceptable and permittable discharge locations and capacities in order to assess the feasibility or 
practicality for central sewer systems.  In most cases, obtaining a discharge permit will require 
proving to MDE that the nutrient (nitrogen and phosphorous), bacterial, or sediment loads will be 
reduced compared to current conditions and that they will not exceed TMDL allocations. 
Furthermore, the reductions need to be lower than current conditions for future load increases 
associated with future growth of county households.  These load reductions will benefit the 
Chesapeake Bay watershed and help Wicomico County meet its goals and objectives under the 
Phase III Watershed Implementation Program (WIP III), as well as reduction requirements related 
to the MDE MS4 permits.

Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
 
4-15 
As a rule, all proposed wastewater treatment plants should be prepared to produce a clean water 
effluent meeting the standards under Enhanced Nutrient Removal (ENR).  This standard is the 
highest level of treatment.  It results in reducing the concentration of Total N to 4 mg/l and reducing 
the concentration of Total P to 0.3 mg/l.  Two (2) type of discharge permits may be obtained from 
MDE, namely: 
 
Surface Water Discharge (Under the EPA NPDES program) 
Groundwater Discharge (Under the State of Maryland program) 
 
A Surface Water Discharge permit is administered by MDE and conforms to EPA rules and 
regulations under the National Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (NPDES).  The municipal 
surface water discharge permit is a combined state and federal permit under the National Pollutant 
Discharge Elimination System (NPDES).  This permit is issued for sewage treatment plants and 
some water treatment plants that discharge to State surface waters.  The permit is designed to 
protect the quality of the body of water receiving the discharge.  Anyone who discharges 
wastewater to surface waters may need a surface water discharge permit.  Applicants include 
municipalities, counties, federal facilities, schools, and commercial water and wastewater 
treatment plants, as well as treatment systems for private residences that discharge to surface 
waters.  Once a completed application is submitted, it could take 9 to 12 months for approval.  The 
treatment requirements will either be based upon technical requirements or water quality standards 
for the receiving water.   
 
The basic approach to demonstrating that a surface water discharge of nutrients will not negatively 
impact a receiving water is to make the case that removing OSDS and their nutrient discharge into 
groundwater and ultimately to a receiving water body will result in a net reduction of nutrient 
impact.  MDE has promulgated guidelines for assessing the nitrogen reduction for replacing OSDS 
with central sewers and ENR treatment levels.  The nitrogen removal credits are as follows:  
 
OSDS within Critical Areas   
 
= 18.56 pounds Nitrogen/year/OSDS 
OSDS within 1,000 feet of a waterbody 
= 11.6 pounds Nitrogen/year/OSDS 
 
All other OSDS  
 
 
 
= 6.96 pounds Nitrogen/year/OSDS

A Groundwater Discharge Permit applies to the disposal of treated municipal or industrial 
wastewater into the State’s ground water via spray irrigation and other land-treatment applications 
as well as into the subsurface by a drain field.  A groundwater discharge permit will contain the 
limitations and requirements deemed necessary to protect public health and protect ground water 
quality.  The new permits take about 9 months to obtain after submission of a completed 
application.  The permits are valid for a period of 5 years.  Renewal permits take approximately 
14 months.    
 
Groundwater discharge permits are desirable in that some additional treatment can occur as treated 
wastewater travels through unsaturated soils.  However, the key factor is “unsaturated soils” and 
due to the low surface elevations and high ground water tables as well as perched high water tables 
D R A F T

<!-- page 130 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
4-18 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
in many areas, it is often difficult to find and secure suitable land in large quantities to make 
groundwater discharge practical and affordable compared to a surface water discharge.   
 
The following sections of this Chapter 4 will discuss each of the nineteen (19) service districts and 
discuss a potential future path to accommodate population growth in accordance with 
comprehensive plans and accommodate households with aging and failed OSDS.  The discussion 
will be broken down into the three types of categories.   First will be a discussion of the ICTs, 
second will be a discussion of the rural villages, and third will be a discussion of community 
subdivisions.   
 
4.3.1 Incorporated Cities and Towns (ICT) 
4.3.1.1  Sharptown  
The proposed technological approach in the Sharptown service district is to extend sewer and 
municipal boundaries to address problems with aging and failing onsite septic disposal systems.  
Sixty (60) parcels with OSDS have been identified.  This approach is consistent with Town and 
State planning and current Tier mapping.  Increasing the discharge capacity of the Town’s WWTP 
could be necessary and should be justified and approved by MDE based upon removal of pollutant 
discharges to groundwater from OSDS.  The County should take an active interest and supporting 
role to the extent requested by Sharptown and within the financial and manpower resources 
available.  It is advocated that the County and Sharptown be open to exploring alternative 
management strategies that range from the County assisting with operations and management, to 
the County taking over full responsibility for utility sewer services.  This is not intended to 
minimize the myriad of other issues impacted by a cooperative agreement between the County and 
Sharptown.  It only suggests that future cost-effectiveness of utility sewer service to the Sharptown 
resident might be enhanced by the County taking over operations.   
 
The WWTP serving Sharptown has not received a major upgrade in many years.  Soon Sharptown 
will need to consider making major improvements due to the aging condition of existing structures, 
as well as upgrading the treatment level for enhanced nutrient removal.  Upgrading the treatment 
levels for ENR treatment would be eligible to receive grant funds from MDE under the Bay 
D R A F T

<!-- page 131 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
 
4-19 
Restoration program, for the existing rated plant capacity.  An increase in flow capacity could be 
funded with loan funds through MDE’s or USDA’s water and wastewater programs.   
 
The San Domingo community is approximately 3 miles from Sharptown and has aging OSDS, 
some of which are failing.  It is outside the proposed growth area of Sharptown but has the potential 
for needing central sewer service.  The more straightforward technical approach and more likely 
cost-effective approach would involve connecting this community to the Sharptown WWTP.   
 
4.3.1.2  Mardela Springs 
The extent of problems related to aging and failing OSDS in the municipality of Mardela Springs 
point to a central sewer utility system as the most appropriate technological approach.  In the past, 
preliminary engineering studies have been completed and cost estimates prepared, but the 
magnitude of costs and implementation hurdles have been beyond the capability of Mardela 
Springs to successfully overcome.  The suggested technological approach for this area would be 
to increase the number of initial users to the sewer system, adding houses outside the current 
municipal limits, and include provisions to accommodate wastewater from Northwestern 
Elementary School and the proposed new Mardela High School.  Increasing the size and scope of 
an initial central sewer system is intended to achieve an economy of scale to lower annual sewer 
utility bills.  Increasing the scope is also intended to achieve a greater environmental benefit by 
removing more OSDS from discharging pollutants to groundwater.  There is an existing surface 
water discharge into Barren Creek for Mardela High School under a permit issued to the Wicomico 
County Board of Education.  Discontinuance of this permit and treating this wastewater at an 
alternate downstream location would provide an environmental benefit.  The current annual cost 
and expenses incurred by the County Board of Education could be more cost-effectively used to 
support a larger central sewer system.    
 
The overall cost and economic hurdles will still be formidable.  However, as a project achieves a 
greater environmental benefit, it should be in a better position to garner more grant funding 
support.  It is anticipated that all municipal central sewer systems built within the County will 
require some degree of grant funding from State and/or Federal sources.   The amount of grant 
funding from USDA is based upon a calculation intended to achieve a level of affordability for 
D R A F T

<!-- page 132 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
4-20 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
users.  A proposed project as outlined herein has been submitted to MDE for the purpose of seeking 
input on potential state funding.   
 
4.3.1.3  Hebron  
The Hebron WWTP was built 40 years ago and uses a lagoon system for treatment.   This technical 
approach is the one of the simplest to operate and maintain but is not very adaptable to achieve 
higher degrees of treatment and nutrient removals.  Soon Hebron will need to address making a 
major investment in the WWTP to serve potential growth scenarios within its municipal 
boundaries.  The Town policy requires annexation as a condition to receive water or sewer service.   
Within the County growth area surrounding Hebron, 138 OSDS were identified.  Taking these 
OSDS systems offline and connecting them to an ENR treatment plant would qualify this 
infrastructure for grant funding.    
 
The preferred technological approach for failing OSDS in the County growth areas would be 
connecting these households to the Hebron sewer system, and subsidize Hebron for increasing the 
capacity and level of treatment at the WWTP.      
 
The assessment of aging and failing OSDS did not reveal a high-level severity to suggest central 
sewer service is an immediate priority.  Subdivisions in the surrounding areas are generally not 
old and often have lots sizes around one-half acre or larger and afford an opportunity for fixing 
and upgrading the OSDS.  No immediate action is recommended at this time.   
 
The extent and nature of a collaboration between the County and Hebron to provide utility sewer 
service is something that should be revisited from time to time as circumstances warrant.   
 
4.3.1.4  Delmar 
The Town of Delmar and the City of Salisbury installed the earliest central sewer systems in 
Wicomico County going back over 100 years.  The Town of Delmar has participated with the 
County in several successful Urban Service Districts going back about 50 years.   The municipal 
boundaries of Delmar in Maryland are somewhat convoluted in the sense that various pipestem 
D R A F T

<!-- page 133 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
 
4-21 
type annexations have created pockets of County land within the overall Town boundary.  Current 
Town policy requires annexation as a condition of water or sewer utility.  
 
The Towns of Delmar MD-DE have created a Utilities Commission to operate and manage the 
water and wastewater systems in both Towns.  It has proven successful and financially stable to 
generally be proactive in meeting the needs of these utilities.  The current design capacity of the 
WWTP is 850,000 gpd and meets ENR treatment standards.  The current population would be 
expected to produce less than 600,000 gpd of wastewater.  However, Delmar is experiencing a 
problem with available wastewater treatment capacity because the old and aging sewers are 
allowing groundwater infiltration and stormwater inflow to an extent that daily average flows 
approach full plant design capacity.  Delmar needs to rehabilitate or replace leaking sewers as a 
precondition to adding new users.  This Master Plan identified 671 OSDS in the County within the 
overall Town boundary or in the County growth area bordering the western Town boundary.   
These households could contribute approximately 170,000 gpd of additional flow to the WWTP.  
The Town has successfully provided sewers into newly annexed areas and is expected to do so 
into the future expanding westward. 
 
In the meeting with Delmar officials, it was learned that the Town does not have a strong interest 
in expansion eastward. The Master Plan identified 146 existing OSDS in the Town Transition 
growth area shown in the County Comprehensive Plan along the eastern Town border.  Therefore, 
if the County allows growth in this unincorporated area, the County will need to participate in the 
building or operation of a central sewer system.  Under the guiding principle that growth “pays for 
growth,” the developers of future subdivisions would be required to fund the capital cost for 
construction.  Under the guiding principle that prohibits private ownership and operation of 
wastewater facilities serving multiple land parcels, the County would be required to take over the 
operations and management of the system.  For the Master Plan, this area has been classified as 
the East Delmar Community subdivision service district and will not be a part of the Town of 
Delmar service district.   
 
 
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 134 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
4-22 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
4.3.1.5  City of Salisbury  
The proposed Salisbury Service District in the Master Plan closely follows the Municipal Growth 
Element for future growth in the Comprehensive Plan for the City of Salisbury that was approved 
in 2017.  From a technology perspective, it represents the most cost-effective solution and the most 
environmentally beneficial solution to the citizens of Wicomico County long term.     
 
MDE OSDS regulations are unsuitable, impractical, and are not sustainable long term.  Soil 
conditions often have a high groundwater table, combined with small lot sizes.  They often do not 
provide adequate spacing between replacement systems and property lines, or onsite wells, or 
neighboring onsite wells.  The impracticality and expense of OSDS repairs and replacement have 
caused home values to decrease and, in some cases, rendered homes uninhabitable.  Subsequently, 
it creates a downward economic cycle and takes a toll on the economic well-being of both the City 
and County residents. MDE OSDS regulation cannot remedy the extent and severity of these 
problems.   The housing market is crippled and will remain so until a viable long-term solution is 
selected for implementation.   
 
Community drain fields are not viable due to the overall housing density within the Routs 13 
Bypass.   County management of OSDS with full time operators pumping out non-draining septic 
tanks and operating a central septage handling and disposal facility is not cost-effective long term.  
It also creates an unacceptable situation with septage hauling trucks traveling throughout urban 
streets and neighborhoods, when the potential number of OSDS in these areas is estimated to 
approach 5,000 dwellings.    
 
Unfortunately, many of these urban subdivisions and residential areas have been ignored for far 
too long and the failure to take any action is reflected in the County 10-year Comprehensive Water 
and Sewer Plan.  Reports commissioned by Wicomico County as far back as 1979 identified 
problems with failing OSDS and pointed to the need to install central sewer.  Now, 40 years later, 
very little has been constructed to address the problem.  Too many areas in the Comprehensive 
Water and Sewer have been categorized as S-6, meaning nothing is being planned or considered 
for the next 10 years.  The next update of the County 10-year Comprehensive Water and Sewer 
Plan, as required by the State of Maryland, must address the installation of central sewer as soon 
D R A F T

<!-- page 135 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
 
4-23 
as possible.  The issues and ramifications of annexing County households into the City of Salisbury 
do not negate the obvious best technological solution.  Central sewers connected to the Salisbury 
WWTP is the best technological option and are beyond the purvey of the engineering scope of this 
Master Plan.    
 
It is not practical or cost-effective for the County to consider providing central water or sewer 
service to these urban subdivisions.  The County does not have a location or permit from MDE to 
dispose of clean water after advanced treatment.  Installing two (2) separate sewer lines and two 
(2) separate water lines in the same street right-of-way, one set for the County and one set for the 
City, would create more cost, more problems, and decrease the quality of services to all utility 
customers.      
 
Some of the aging and failing County OSDS systems are located within the recharge areas for 
Salisbury in either the Park well field or the Paleo well field.  These OSDS present a potential risk 
as a source of contamination to public water supply wells.  There should be motivation for 
Salisbury to help take OSDS offline as a protective measure against future well contamination.   In 
a recent request for proposal from the City, the evidence of nitrogen in some of the wells in the 
Paleo Well field was acknowledged  The City is undertaking engineering planning for possible 
installation of nitrogen removal filters, at some future date, if necessary.   
 
The wastewater treatment plant serving Salisbury has a design capacity of 8.5 mgd and has some 
ability to take in additional users in accordance with the City Comprehensive Plan.  This project 
was partially funded by MDE from EPA and Maryland’s Bay Restoration Fund.  To the extent that 
the WWTP capacity is available for users outside current City boundaries, and to the extent the 
capacity was paid for with public funds, it may be unrealistic for the County to expect to receive 
grant money for a separate County WWTP.   
 
In conclusion, the recommended technological option is central sewers in the Salisbury Service 
District connected to the Salisbury WWTP.   
 
 
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 136 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
4-24 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
4.3.1.6  City of Fruitland  
Around 2002, Wicomico County financially assisted with the upgrade and expansion of the City 
of Fruitland WWTP to achieve Biological Nutrient Removal (BNR).  This occurred around the 
time the Fruitland Walmart and Texas Roadhouse opened, which brought jobs and income tax 
revenue to the County.  In return for the financial assistance, the County was allowed access to 
200,000 gpd capacity from Fruitland’s WWTP and water system, under an Urban Service 
agreement.  The County accessed a portion of this capacity with the opening of Bennett Middle 
School.  After that, the County accessed a portion of Fruitland’s water system capacity to alleviate 
the toxic water contamination in the Morris Mill area.  Within this arrangement of allowing County 
access to water and sewer capacity, the City of Fruitland retained the right to require annexation 
as a condition to serving County areas.    
 
The preferred technical solution for septic disposal system problems within the Fruitland growth 
area is central sewers and connection to the Fruitland WWTP.  This preferred solution comes with 
the caveat that Fruitland, with County assistance, will need to acquire TMDL discharge approval 
from MDE for continuation of the surface water discharge when the WWTP reaches full design 
capacity of 800,000 gpd.  If the City and County cannot obtain the additional surface water 
discharge capacity, land application or reclaimed water reuse will need to be investigated.  Based 
upon future growth projections for the current municipal boundaries of Fruitland, the discharge 
capacity will need to be increased by 700,000 gpd to a total of 1.5 mgd.   
 
Around 15 years ago, Fruitland undertook an investigation and identified significant infiltration 
and inflow (I/I) in the sewer collection system.  Fruitland proceeded with corrective measures to 
successfully eliminate over 100,000 gpd of I/I.  This reduction in I/I allows the WWTP to serve an 
additional 400 residential connections.  At the present time, Fruitland has again initiated an I/I 
investigation with the intent of rehabilitating defects in the sewer collection system and increase 
available capacity at the WWTP.   
 
Fruitland Service District dwellings outside Fruitland limits at full build-out were estimated to be 
approximately 760 and represent a flow of 190,000 gpd.  Identifiable residential housing and 
D R A F T

<!-- page 137 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
 
4-25 
neighborhoods that currently or in the future might experience problems and issues with OSDS or 
individual wells include the following: 
 
▪ Tony Tank  
▪ Timberlake  
▪ Cherry Hill  
▪ Moonglow Road Area  
▪ Bohnak Manufactured Home Park (MHP) 
 
Tony Tank and Timberlake subdivisions and the Moonglow Road area border the municipal 
boundary of Fruitland and have OSDS and individual wells.  Aging and failing septic systems 
create problems for individual residences of varying degrees of severity and expense.  Nutrient 
and sediment impairment of Tony Tank Pond and Tony Tank Lake was identified by MDE in 1996 
and documented in the EPA approved TMDL report in 1999 (see List of References for a link to 
this report).  Current funding from the Maryland Bay Restoration program indicates that a grant 
of approximately $20,000 per individual OSDS could be available to connect to Fruitland’s 
Enhanced Nutrient Removal (ENR) WWTP.    
 
As OSDS problems in the Fruitland Service District are brought to the attention of the Health 
Department and Wicomico County, the preferred technical solution is a central sewer collection 
system with treatment at the Fruitland WWTP.    
 
4.3.1.7  Town of Pittsville  
The WWTP serving Pittsville is treating daily flows at or near the design capacity of 115,000 gpd, 
and it is not able to receive wastewater flow from County areas nearby its municipal boundary.  
Pittsville has a policy that requires annexation as a precondition for connection to its water or 
wastewater systems.  Furthermore, the WWTP needs major repairs and replacements to continue 
to function at its current rated flow capacity.  Any increase to the WWTP capacity will trigger an 
increased level of treatment from the BNR level to the ENR level.  MDE has grant funding 
available for upgrading the treatment level from BNR to ENR through the Bay Restoration Fund. 
However, the grant funding comes with the caveat that limits the amount of grant to the existing 
D R A F T

<!-- page 138 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
4-26 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
flow capacity of 115,000 gpd.  Alternate sources of funding, such as the MDE Clean Water 
Revolving Loan Fund, would be needed to increase the plant flow capacity.  Increasing the level 
of treatment at the WWTP will reduce the nutrient loadings discharged to Aydelotte Branch and 
ultimately into the Upper Pocomoke watershed.  Under this scenario, MDE could permit an 
increase in flow capacity at the higher treatment level, provided there is net overall reduction of 
nutrients being discharged.   
 
Pittsville faces significant financial challenges with operations and maintenance of its utility 
systems at affordable rates.  This presents opportunities for consideration of financial assistance 
from the County in return for helping the County address problems with OSDS systems.  The 
County’s Comprehensive Plan has designated the land east of Pittsville along Route 346 and 
extending to Willards under the Town Transition land use category.  There appears to be little 
interest on the part of Pittsville or Willards to actively expand its municipal boundaries to serve 
these areas.  As a consequence, the Master Plan identified this area as the East Wicomico 
Community Service District with the understanding the County would take the lead in responding 
to the need for central water or sewer services.   
 
4.3.1.8  The Town of Willards  
At the present, there are no significant subdivisions or clusters of homes with aging or failing 
OSDS to warrant extending central sewer outside the current municipal boundaries.  The use of an 
urban service agreement to extend sewer to County areas is not an option as Willards maintains a 
policy that annexation will be required.    
 
Within the past 12 years, Willards completed major wastewater and water projects.  The 
wastewater plant was upgraded and increased capacity to 200,000 gpd, and a new water plant was 
constructed to treat 240,000 gpd.  Recently a third water supply well was constructed to 
supplement the two existing wells.  The outlook for Willards is that the available wastewater and 
water capacity should be adequate to support infill development within the current municipal 
corporate boundary.   
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 139 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
 
4-27 
Based upon the TMDL load limits associated with wastewater discharge to Burnt Mill Branch, it 
is doubtful that Willards could get approval for increasing flows at the WWTP to serve newly 
annexed areas.  Preliminary efforts to locate suitable, well drained soils for disposal by wastewater 
land application as an adjunct to surface water discharge have been unsuccessful.    
 
The area along Route 346 west of Willards has been designated as “Town Transition” in the 
County Comprehensive Plan.  This area which is labeled the East Wicomico Community Service 
District was included in a 2009 Wastewater Treatment Study undertaken by Pittsville.  The study 
looked at combining the wastewater treatment plants for Willards and Pittsville under one surface 
water discharge permit, with a total flow capacity of 500,000 gpd.  At the time, Bay Restoration 
Grant funding was limited to larger wastewater treatment plants that have at least 500,000 gpd 
flow capacity.  The County did not actively participate in the study, and Towns did not move 
forward with efforts to combine their wastewater systems.   
 
As a small town with a small customer base, Willards is challenged to keep the sewer and water 
rates at levels high enough to fully support all operation and maintenance expenses for a long-term 
sustainable utility system service.  Willards has been proactive in developing “impact fees” and 
budgeting this revenue to support future capital expenses.    
 
While Willards does not have any immediate plans or needs to increase water supply or wastewater 
capacity, they expressed a willingness to continue discussion with Wicomico County and 
cooperate where mutually beneficial to both parties.    
 
4.3.1.9  Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin Village  
Due to their proximity to each other, the villages of Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin were evaluated as 
one potential utility sewer district, rather than three (3) separate or independent utility sewer 
districts. From a technology standpoint, one (1) treatment plant in place of three (3) treatment 
plants would be significantly less costly to operate and sustain.  
 
From an engineering and technical perspective, the use of OSDS as a long-term solution for the 
protection of public health or environmental protection is problematic.  As groundwater levels 
D R A F T

<!-- page 140 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
4-28 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
come closer to the surface in the villages due to tidal fluctuations and sea level rise, the number of 
failing or poor functioning OSDS will increase.  Even with extensive or mandatory use of Best 
Available Technology (BAT) OSDS, the levels of total nitrogen and total phosphorous removal 
and reduction in bacterial would be less than what would be achieved with an ENR WWTP.  
 
A total of 640 dwellings were identified in the three villages. Almost half of the dwellings were in 
the Maryland Critical Area.  OSDS in the Critical Area have the greatest negative impact to the 
watershed.  An annual nitrogen loading impact to the Nanticoke watershed was calculated at of 
8,227 pounds.  If there was one plant serving all dwellings performing at Enhanced Nutrient 
Removal (ENR), the expected annual nitrogen loading could be reduced to under 2,500 pounds.   
The estimate of below 2,500 pounds Total Nitrogen (TN) was calculated as shown below:  
 
 0.20 mgd @ 4 mg/l TN x 8.34 (conv factor) x 365 days per year = 2,435 pounds Total N  
 
Using a typical household flow of 250 gpd/EDU and 640 dwellings, the expected wastewater flow 
would be 160,000 gpd.  A treatment plant designed to treat 200,000 gpd could accommodate 800 
EDU’s.  
 
The number of OSDS systems located in the Maryland Critical Area that could be taken offline 
would make this area a high priority to MDE and improve its chances for receiving grant funding.    
 
Currently, there does not exist an existing problem of great significance or an imminent directive 
from MDE or the County Health Department to initiate taking the OSDS offline.  Accordingly, 
the Master Plan is not recommending immediate planning and preliminary engineering for a 
central sewer utility system.  However, finding the most environmentally acceptable method and 
location for treated wastewater discharge or land application is critical to the overall feasibility of 
a central sewer utility system.  We recommend that the County begin the process of finding and 
acquiring approval from MDE for a surface water discharge and seek a suitable location for land 
application of treated wastewater.  We believe the treatment level before discharge would basically 
be the same for both options.  It may be practical that land application of treated wastewater could 
be limited to the vegetative growing season and only applied at consumptive use agronomic rates.   
D R A F T

<!-- page 141 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 4: Water and Sewer Infrastructure 
 
4-29 
Under this scenario, there would still be a need for a surface water discharge during the winter 
non-growing season.    
 
4.3.1.10 Whitehaven Village  
Whitehaven Village is a very small village with less than 50 homes.  It is located along the 
Wicomico River and is entirely within the Maryland Critical Area.  The groundwater table as at or 
near the ground surface most of the year.  The streets experience tidal flooding monthly.  The 
village is entirely unsuitable for OSDS and they should be taken offline as soon as possible.   
Valiant efforts by the County Health Department to work with homeowners with OSDS repairs, 
replacements and experimental systems are not sufficient for protection of public health or 
environmental protection.  A recent example was the issuance of a permit for an onsite Mound 
System with a maximum hydraulic capacity of 60 gallons per day because was occupied by only 
one resident.

Chapter 5: 
Management - Governance 
 
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 164 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Management - Governance 
 
5-1 
Chapter 5:  Management – Governance  
5.1  Introduction  
The current management-governance approach for providing water and sewer services by 
Wicomico County is the Urban Service Commission (USC) as authorized in the County Code – 
Chapter 97.  In oversimplified & broad terms, the USC Code prescribes rules and policies used by 
the County for the creation of water or sewer urban service district and construction of the 
infrastructure and entering into an agreement for supply or treatment from the nearby municipality.  
(USC also can provide streetlights, storm water, sidewalks, curbs, and gutters but these 
components are not pertinent to the Water & Sewer Master Plan). The USC Code does not 
preclude the County from actively building and operating its own water or wastewater treatment 
plant(s).  However, we note the term “urban” is used, and believe it may be intentional, implying 
service by the nearby municipality.  In the 1960’s, the municipalities did not require annexation; 
today annexation is a mandatory condition for service.  The municipalities, through their 
comprehensive plan, have made it known they may wish to grow, and often water and sewer utility 
service is a key factor favoring annexation.     
 
When transitioning from a rural environment to an urban environment, there is a need for the 
County to become actively involved in providing full water and sewer utility service, given the 
age of individual on-site systems, which too often cannot be upgraded or modified to satisfy ever 
increasing MDE rules and regulations.   
 
This Chapter will assess the County USC Code 97, created 50 years ago, for its adaptability to 
meet today’s water and sewer challenges.  It will also present alternate management-governance 
approaches, presenting the pros and cons of each, with the intention that the County Council decide 
and select the best and most appropriate option.  The assessment of alternate governance models 
and determining which governance model best suits the needs in Wicomico County, needs to 
include both the near term as the utility gets established and the long term as the utility grows and 
matures.   The management/governance model chosen needs to be an integral component along 
with the technologies employed in the physical infrastructure and along with the 
financial/accounting component to have the most successful and sustainable water and sewer 
utility systems.   
D R A F T

<!-- page 165 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
5-2 
 
Management - Governance 
 
The Master Plan evaluated three (3) types of management/governance systems.   First, was the 
current Urban Service District (USD) system contained in the Wicomico County Code.    Second, 
was an approach whereby the County creates a separate Division for Water-Sewer Utilities 
operating under Department of Public Works using rate-payer enterprise funds to build, own, 
operate, maintain, and manage water – sewer infrastructure.  This second approach, as the customer 
base grows, could transition into a separate Department of Water within the County Government.   
The third model examined was the creation of an autonomous or semi-autonomous governmental 
unit, sometimes referred to as an Authority or Sanitary District or Utility Commission.  In this 
Master Plan, the term Water Authority is intended to encompass all variations of the concept, 
such as, a sanitary district, water district, sewer authority, etc.   
 
A survey was made of the management-governance systems in the 23 counties in Maryland, to get 
an overview of the models used throughout.  Before evaluating management/governance models 
specific to Wicomico County a review was made of other County Systems, we looked at how other 
Maryland Counties approached water and sewer services.   First, we compiled a listing of the type 
of local County governance for each county as shown in Table 5-1.   We found that six (6) County 
governments operate under a “Commission” legislation such as Somerset.   There are twelve (12) 
counties such as Wicomico and Dorchester which operate under a “Charter” form; and five (5) 
counties such as Worcester which operates under a “Code” legal structure.

While Wicomico County does not provide day to day operation and maintenance of water or sewer 
utilities; the County does in fact handle the creation of utility districts and provides upfront funding 
and manages the construction.   An example would be the recently created Morris Mill Urban 
Service Water District which provided an $8 Million water distribution system serving County 
residents in the Morris Mill area east of Fruitland.

utility service.  These Counties were
