5.2  Urban Service Commission  
The current County governance approach for the creation and implementation of centralized water 
and sewerage service districts is provided for in the Wicomico County Code under Chapter 97 – 
URBAN SERVICE DISTRICTS.(25)  It provided for the creation of the Urban Services 
D R A F T

<!-- page 169 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
5-6 
 
Management - Governance 
Commission (USC) and is not limited to just water and sewer infrastructure. The USC can also 
sponsor projects involving street lighting, curbing, and guttering, storm drainage and surface water 
drainage, fire protection, police protection and garbage and trash collection.   
 
The Urban Service Commission has been in existence since the 1960’s.   A Master USD agreement 
was executed between the County and individual municipalities with a term of approximately 30 
years.  As water and sewer infrastructure was constructed in a newly created service district, the 
new USD was added to the master agreement.  The earliest urban services agreements for water 
and sewer were between the County and the City of Salisbury and the Town of Delmar.  Prior to 
the 1960’s, Salisbury and Delmar were the only municipalities in the County providing central 
services.   The County would generally bill each user once per year to pay their share of debt 
service on the bonds used to build the system.  This billing was based on Ad Valorem taxes or 
benefit assessments.  This would continue through the life of the bond (typically 20 or 30 years).  
After the bonds were retired, the County would no longer bill the user.  The ownership of the 
buried infrastructure transferred to the municipality who would operate and maintain and repair 
the infrastructure in perpetuity.     
 
Through the course of creation of individual urban service districts, the County was able to make 
contractual arrangements for water supply and wastewater treatment and thereby avoid the 
necessity of hiring added County staff for operations, maintenance, repairs, along with staff for 
associated support departments for billings, HR, payroll, and engineering.    
 
Creation of urban service districts can follow one of three (3) procedures.  A general outline of 
each procedure is provided herein in italics.  
 
Service Districts can be created by one of three (3) procedures:  
 
1. By action of the Urban Services Commission 
2. By petition of 20 property owners within an area to the County  
3. By emergency order from the Maryland Department of the Environment (MDE) and/or 
Health Department (MDH). 
D R A F T

<!-- page 170 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Management - Governance 
 
5-7 
 
5.2.1  Unilateral Action by the USC 
The Urban Service Commission can unilaterally initiate the process of establishing a service 
district as follows from the USC Code: 
 
“Commission shall first determine the exact boundaries of the area 
requiring such service and shall then cause surveys to be made of the 
area and a complete engineering and financial report prepared on the 
proposed service which shall include the estimated assessed property 
valuation as fixed for county taxation within the proposed subdistrict, 
which estimated assessed valuation shall be furnished the Commission 
by the county. When such surveys and estimates are completed, the 
Commission shall give notice, by publication in one or more 
newspapers published within the county, once a week for three 
successive weeks, and by handbills posted and circulated in the 
proposed subdistrict, which notice shall specify the boundaries of the 
subdistrict and the urban service to be provided therein, together with 
the time and place of hearing to be held, at which hearing those present 
shall be informed of the contents of the engineering and financial 
surveys and the probable cost of providing the service in the 
subdistrict, including all fees and expenses. The Commission shall 
advise those present at the hearing whether, on the basis of the 
engineering and financial surveys, it believes the project is feasible 
from both engineering and financial standpoints. The finding of fact and 
the decision of the Commission shall be final unless within 45 days after 
the date of said hearing a petition signed by the owners of 20% of the 
assessed valuation of the land (excluding the improvements thereon) 
lying within the proposed service area is filed with the Commission 
requesting a vote on the proposal. Upon receipt of a petition signed by 
the owners of 20% of the assessed valuation of the land lying within 
the proposed subdistrict requesting a vote on the proposal, the 
Commission shall set a time and place for the balloting, which place 
shall be within the proposed subdistrict.” (25) 
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 171 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
5-8 
 
Management - Governance 
5.2.2  Petition of Twenty (20) Property Owners  
Under this process for creation of a service district, the request is made by a minimum of twenty 
(20) property owners who also must define the boundaries of the proposed district.  If the request 
is approved, the Commission will undertake surveys and feasibilities studies, at County expense 
and follow the provisions of the USC Code as follows:  
“By petition. Whenever 20 property owners residing in any locality in 
Wicomico County or, if there are not 20 property owners in such locality, 
then 25% of the owners therein shall petition the Commission to 
provide a specified urban service or services within an area (the 
boundaries whereof shall be definitely stated in said petition), the 
Commission, if it approves, shall cause to be made a preliminary 
engineering survey of the project to determine the feasibility and 
probable cost of providing such area with the urban service or services 
requested in said petition. The Commission shall prepare without cost 
to the petitioners a preliminary estimate to determine the approximate 
cost of providing such facilities. The Commission shall also submit an 
estimate of the cost of making surveys and preparing a complete 
engineering and financial report which shall include the estimated 
assessed property valuation as fixed for county taxation purposes 
within the proposed subdistrict, which estimated assessed valuation 
shall be furnished to the Commission by the county. Whenever such 
preliminary estimates are completed… shall give notice…which notice 
shall state the boundaries of the subdistrict and the urban service to be 
provided therein, together with the time and place of the hearing to be 
held…At the conclusion of the hearing, the Commission shall determine 
whether, on the basis of the preliminary surveys and estimates of the 
probable cost of providing the urban service or services requested and 
the facts and comments presented by the residents of the area during 
the hearing, it should have a complete engineering and financial report 
made to determine the feasibility of providing the urban service or 
services requested. If the Commission decides that the proposed service 
is desirable for the health, safety and welfare of the residents of the 
proposed subdistrict and seems feasible from both the engineering and 
financial standpoints, it shall proceed to have complete engineering 
and financial reports prepared, including the estimated assessed 
property valuation as fixed for county taxation within the proposed 
subdistrict. If the Commission decides not to proceed, its decision shall 
be final unless within 45 days after the date of said hearing a petition 
signed by the owners of 25% of the assessed valuation of the land 
(excluding improvements thereon) lying within the proposed subdistrict 
is filed with the Commission requesting a vote on the proposal. Upon 
receipt of a petition signed by the owners of 25% of the assessed 
valuation of the land lying within the proposed subdistrict requesting a 
vote on the proposal, the Commission shall set a time and place for the 
balloting, which place shall be within the proposed subdistrict.” (25)  
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 172 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Management - Governance 
 
5-9 
The approach to a USC initiated service district compared to a property owner petition request are 
generally similar, but do have a distinction.  For example, the USC initiated approach procedure 
requires a petition of 20% of affected properties to call for a ballot referendum to oppose a USC 
decision to proceed with a project.   For an Owner petition request, there needs to be a second 
petition of 25% of affected properties to challenge a decision by the USC to turn down a requested 
project.   
 
5.2.3  By Emergency Order from MDE and/or MDH  
The third approach for creation of a USC service district may come from an emergency order 
issued by MDE or the Maryland Department of Health.  An example where this might occur was 
the presence of a toxic chemical contamination of individual water supply wells rendering the 
water dangerous for public health.   Another would be a preponderance of failing OSDS’s creating 
surface contamination and a public health disease risk.   If directed by MDE or MDH, the Urban 
Service Commission must proceed with providing the necessary infrastructure with the service 
district, regardless of public comment or acceptance, unless the County can prove that the 
necessary funds cannot be obtained.  If an emergency order is issued by MDE or MDH, we believe 
it is much more likely than not, that the State will provide grant or loan funds and make an earnest 
effort see a project through fruition.    
 
As described above the County has the ability through existing Code and the district to provide 
water and sewer to County residents.  It would not be incorrect to say that the County is in the 
water and sewer business.  Up to this point, the County has been able to contract with 
municipalities to take over operation and maintenance and treatment as a more efficient and 
effective service.  This approach, over the years, has been able to eliminate overlap and 
unnecessary redundancy.  
 
However, within the Metro (Delmar-Salisbury-Fruitland) corridor many years have come and gone 
since the of addition of new urban service district.   The ages of onsite systems in many areas are 
past the typical useful life of septic disposal systems which is about 30 years.    As stated elsewhere, 
many onsite systems within the Metro corridor, within the Route 13 bypass and contiguous to 
D R A F T

<!-- page 173 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
5-10 
 
Management - Governance 
municipalities, are past 50 years of use and are immediate candidates for central sewer systems at 
a minimum, and preferably both water and sewer systems.   
 
5.2.4  Should the Urban Services Commission under County Code 97 be Maintained?  
While the provisions under County Code 97-17 and 97-18 do allow the Urban Services 
Commission to acquire existing public systems, this procedure has not been implemented or tested 
in the 50 years of the Code’s enactment.    Based upon meetings by the County with the seven (7) 
municipalities who own and operate water and sewerage systems within the County it is unlikely 
that they will entertain a County takeover without extensive discussions and negotiations to fashion 
a mutually trusting and beneficial arrangement.   Two of the bigger items would be a utility system 
that assures lower costs over the long term and allows the Towns to maintain control of growth 
through annexation.  The Urban Services Commission as it exists today, without the capability to 
operate and manage water and sewerage facilities, is not positioned to pursue this concept, which 
in the long run, could be mutually beneficial.    As stated earlier, the USC can not only provide 
water and sewer services for public benefit, but can also provide street lighting, curbing, and 
guttering, storm drainage and surface water drainage, fire protection, police protection and garbage 
and trash collection.  If the Master Plan determines and recommends a different management-
governance approach for water and sewer utility service, the other infrastructures services could 
still be viable under County Code 97 and the water and sewer component be deleted through 
legislation. 
 
5.2.5  Rate Structures under an Urban Service District  
The existing Urban Service Districts connecting to municipal water and sewer systems pay user 
rates to the municipality at a multiple of the in-city rate.  Typically, this might be 1.5 times in-city 
rates.    Additionally, the urban service users make an annual payment to the County to pay-off the 
bond indebtedness for the infrastructure.  The annual payment stops when the bonds are paid-off.   
County residents receiving central water and sewer service will pay varying rates for varying 
lengths of time and these rates would not be the same through the County.     
 
If the County elects to continue using County Code 97 and the Urban Services Commission decides 
to build water and sewer systems, inclusive of operations and maintenance of treatment plants, 
D R A F T

<!-- page 174 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Management - Governance 
 
5-11 
wells, pump stations etc.; then the County will need to establish user rates that are specific to each 
Urban Service District that will generate the revenue to be self-sufficient.   Depending upon the 
size and location of a newly created urban service district, the number of users, the clean water 
discharge cost, any additional capacity included, and the extent of grant funding; will result in 
dramatically different user rates among USDs.   Different districts will have differing economies 
of scale and differing discharge costs.   It is not ideal for users of County sponsored water and 
sewer systems to be paying different user rates for essentially the same utility service.   It would 
be a many year until bonds are retired before the County could enact and actualize a uniform rate 
structure.    
 
By way of example, Sussex County, DE has been providing water and sewer utility service since 
the 1970’s to Sussex customers, many of which reside within municipalities.   Each service area 
operated as its own profit center and user rates varied throughout.    After about 20 years, Sussex 
County was able to establish a single uniform “service” charge to all users which covered 
operations and maintenance but not debt service.    After 40 years of existence, Sussex County is 
still striving to create a more uniform rate structure, inclusive of debt service.   Meaning, all Sussex 
County users pay equivalent rates.  New districts and new customers are added to the system user 
rate that is already known.   
 
Somerset County Sanitary District, Inc. (SCSD) is another example where separate water and 
sewer districts in the County were paying different rates.  However, beginning in 2019, the SCSD 
was able to implement a uniform rate per EDU customer Countywide.    
 
Whichever management-governance model the County chooses whether it continues under the 
Urban Service Commission or creates a separate department within the County government, or 
develops enabling legislation for a Water Authority, it will be desirable to achieve a uniform rate 
structure throughout as soon as practical.   
 
5.3  County Water Division or Department of Water 
The second form of management-governance of water and sewerage utility systems could be in 
the form of a Division under the Department of Public Works with future transition to a separate 
D R A F T

<!-- page 175 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
5-12 
 
Management - Governance 
Department of Water (in this context Water Department represents both water and sewer and 
could also include stormwater) within the County.   Noted previously, only nine (9) counties in 
Maryland have an elected County Executive and five (5) of the nine (9) counties provide water 
and sewer utility services within county government as a Division or Department.  Only 
Montgomery and Prince George’s and Baltimore operate under a Water Authority model for water 
and sewer utility services.  As Montgomery, Prince George’s and Baltimore have populations 
much larger than Wicomico, it suggests that a Water Authority might not be the best fit in this 
County.   
 
How would a Water Division or a Department of Water compare or contrast with other 
Departments within the County such as the Sherriff’s Department, the Wicomico County Board of 
Education (WCBOE), or the Department of Corrections or Public Safety?   
 
The County Sheriff’s Office provides police protection within corporate boundaries.  Only three 
municipalities, namely Delmar, Fruitland, and Salisbury, have a Police Department.   The other 
five municipalities (Hebron – Mardela Springs – Pittsville – Sharptown – Willards) and the 
unincorporated areas rely upon the Sheriff’s Department to provide police protection.   Similarly, 
the County, through the Water Division, would provide water and sewer utility services to 
unincorporated areas and could partner or assist municipalities with the operation and maintenance 
of their utility systems.  For example, the County could employ a highly trained and certified 
water/wastewater superintendent, who could oversee the monitoring and reporting and provide 
troubleshooting of problems at treatment plants.  Each town would save money by not having to 
separately hire a highly specialized and certified licensed superintendent.    
 
The County, through the Department of Corrections, and the WCBOE, provide public services not 
provided by any municipality.  These are County branches of government providing public 
benefits to Wicomico County citizens both inside and outside of municipal boundaries.  These 
departments achieve an economy of scale and provide a public benefit in large part by eliminating 
duplication, overlap or redundancy.  Similarly, a goal for the Water Division or Department of 
Water would be an entity having an economy of scale and minimization of redundancy and overlap 
to serve County residents both outside and inside municipal boundaries.     
D R A F T

<!-- page 176 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Management - Governance 
 
5-13 
 
Similarly, a County Department of Water may provide municipal water and sewer system for the 
smaller municipalities and community service districts and village districts, and provide long-term 
operations and maintenance services.  In the unincorporated rural villages and community 
subdivisions, the County will eventually need to be the governmental unit providing central water 
and sewer systems.    
 
A Water Division or Department of Water may not require enabling legislation to create, as would 
be the case if a Water Authority were selected by the County.  This provides the County with 
flexibility in determining the size, scope, and staffing arrangement.   The types of personnel and 
functions needed by a water department might include the following categories:

This breakdown of personnel and staff would need to be proportional to the number of water and

D R A F T

<!-- page 177 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
5-14 
 
Management - Governance 
 
Creation of a Water Division with a mission and objective will have multiple impacts on other 
County Departments to support the Water Division.  These include IT support, HR support, legal 
services, etc. and while this appears to be the same as other departments, there is a unique 
distinction.    
 
This distinction is the service is provided directly to individual customers who will pay a monthly 
or quarterly fee to the County.  The County will set the user rates and the system is intended to 
generate sufficient revenue to meet all expenses.  It will operate as a self-sustaining enterprise 
fund, and the County will need to account for all expenses both within the Water Division and 
across County government directly attributable to providing water and sewer.    
 
In the future, should the Water Division grow in size, decisions will need to be made whether a 
Water Department should be independent and self-sufficient from other County departments.  The 
Water Department would act as a utility service provider not unlike other utility providers such as 
the electric company,  phone company, or cable service company.  It is helpful to keep these utility 
providers in mind, and the typical monthly fees they bill, when considering the monthly bills for 
water and sewer service.  Monthly bills for electric, telephone, and cable service are often in the 
hundreds of dollars per month.   
 
The actual number of employees to staff a water department will increase as the population served 
increases.  At the outset, the challenge will be to make progress with a minimum number of full-
time staff and grow the number of employees will increase as the population served increases.  
 
The Wicomico County Board of Education (WCBOE) was mentioned earlier as a frame of 
reference for a water and sewer utility.  It operates under laws issued at the Federal and State level.  
Similarly, safe drinking water and clean wastewater discharge systems operate under Federal and 
State laws and regulation.  The WCBOE does not charge tuition to students using the schools; 
water and sewer utility systems do charge customers for services rendered.   The WCBOE operates 
and maintains water and sewer infrastructure at some of their schools, including pump stations, 
wastewater treatment plants, septic disposal system drain fields and water filtration systems.  The 
D R A F T

<!-- page 178 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Management - Governance 
 
5-15 
WCBOE has a staff handling operations and maintenance of this infrastructure.  So, indirectly the 
County does employ staff and provides water and utility services within the County.   Should the 
County select to implement a Water Division, it is recommended that the WCBOE 
water/wastewater operations and maintenance staff be transferred from the WCBOE to the newly 
created Water Division.  The last comparison between the WCBOE and a County Department of 
Water is that both services require large capital expenditures, and both are heavily dependent upon 
State and Federal funding support.  These capital expenditures continue as the number of users 
increase and the systems expand in size.  These utility systems age over time, or future regulations 
may require higher degrees of treatment, and technological advances, so the need for capital 
expenditures will continue.    
 
5.3.1  Sussex County Example  
Sussex County (Sussex) Delaware was researched as a model that might offer insights to 
Wicomico County.  Sussex started water and sewer utility services to county residents both inside 
and outside municipal boundaries in the early 1970’s.  It started with one assigned project engineer 
within the Engineering Department and grew in number as projects were completed.  It has grown 
from a Division within the Engineering Department to a separate Department named 
Environmental Services, along with three support Divisions and a Water and Sewer website.   The 
original formation is shown in Table 5-5:  
 
TABLE 5-5:  Initial Management 
Original Water and Sewer Services 
Sussex County Council

Consulting Engineers 
 
 
Sussex hired staff before service was provided to its first water and sewer customer.   Sussex 
taxpayers funded expenses for approximately six years until the first water and sewer billings were 
sent out.  The timing was advantageous for Sussex as the newly created U.S. Environmental 
Protection Agency in 1972 provided planning and design grants of 100%.  Grants for construction

costs were 85% (75% Federal and 10% State).  This illustrates the importance of Federal and State 
to subsidize infrastructure investment.  Throughout the years, Sussex continued building 
infrastructure and adding staff for all facets of utility sewer service. We believe Sussex currently 
serves an estimated 50,000 + customers, based upon information that the systems serve more than 
70,000 EDUs.  This number of customers is larger than the total number of houses within 
Wicomico County.

▪ One-Time System Connection Charge is paid before hook-up and is similar to an Impact 
Fee.  It pays for past expenditures for debt service and a contribution toward future 
expansion or replacement. (currently $6,360.00 per EDU)  
▪ Annual Service Charge recovers the cost of operations, maintenance, electric power, 
personnel, fuel, supplies etc.  Vacant lots do not pay a service charge.  The County has 
established a uniform service charge for all users.  
▪ Annual Assessment Charge recovers the cost of construction and bonded indebtedness 
and is calculated based on a front-footage assessment.  All parcels in the service district, 
including vacant lots must pay the front foot assessment charge.   
 
For many years, the Service Charge was different for each district, and Sussex expended much 
effort in tracking and apportioning costs and charges for each district.  Recently, Sussex was able 
to combine all costs into one county-wide service charge.  The uniform annual service charge is 
approximately $292.00 or $25 per month.

5.3.3  Wicomico – Sussex Comparison  
When comparing the two counties directly, it is important to recognize major differences and 
distinctions between Wicomico and Sussex.  Wicomico County has a land area about one-third the 
size of Sussex and a population that is approximately one-half.  Put another way, Sussex is three 
times larger and two times more populated.  The land area of Sussex County is three times larger 
than Wicomico County and the population is 200,000 to 100,000.  For a balanced comparison, the 
land area and population of Sussex County would be like the three Eastern Shore counties of 
Wicomico-Worcester-Somerset.  Whereby homes built in Wicomico are for full-time residents.  
Thousands of homes in Sussex County are second homes and only occupied seasonally.   Sussex 
has multiple densely populated ocean front resort communities which pay annual fees for seasonal 
service.  It is not an apples-to-apples comparison to reference the number of houses built each year 
in Sussex to the number of houses built annually in Wicomico.  
 
Sussex County no longer has a Department of Public Works; it now functions as the Division of 
Utility Planning and Design Review.  Sussex does not provide solid waste services as all solid 
waste services are provided at the State level.  Sussex does not have a roads department or a police 
department as these services are provided at the State level.  Education and schools are provided 
by individual school districts, not by Sussex government.  However, Sussex does provide the 
billing and collection services for the taxes established by the individual school districts.   
 
Sussex County does not have responsibility for on-site disposal systems.  That responsibility 
belongs to the State through the Department of Natural resources and Environmental Control 
D R A F T

<!-- page 183 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
5-20 
 
Management - Governance 
(DNREC).  DNREC issues the wastewater permits for on-site systems and performs the required 
inspections.  Sussex maintains a Tier mapping system that is comparable to Maryland and 
Wicomico County but includes an additional Tier 5 category for regulated on-site systems areas.    
 
5.4  Water Authority 
5.4.1 Introduction 
A “Water Authority” is the term used in this Master Pan to describe an agency, or commission, or 
utility provider created by Wicomico County through enabling legislation approved by the State 
of Maryland to provide county residents with safe drinking water, and/or wastewater treatment 
and/or stormwater services.     It may be called a Regional Authority, Public Service Authority or 
Sanitary Commission or Sanitary District.  Some examples of a Water Authority in the State of 
Maryland are Easton Utilities, Somerset Sanitary District, Dorchester Sanitary District, the St. 
Mary’s Metropolitan Commission (METCOM), and the Washington Suburban Sanitary 
Commission (WSSC).  A Water Authority is not limited to only providing drinking water; as a 
utility service it can provide any combination of drinking water, clean water, or storm water, even 
electric and cable.    
 
5.4.2  What is a Water Authority?  
A water authority is a not-for-profit public benefit service corporation that reliably provides 
quality, affordable drinking water or clean water for disposal that protects public health, enhances 
environmental protection, and fosters economic vitality to Wicomico county residents and 
communities and rural villages and incorporated Towns who request service.    
 
As envisioned in this Master Plan, a Water Authority would be created by Wicomico County 
through State approved legislation.  Typically, it would involve establishment of a governing 
Board of Directors that represent stakeholders.  The board might be appointed by the County 
Council, County Executive, or individual Towns if they are provided service from the Water 
Authority.  It would operate as a not-for-profit public agency and is intended to be self-funded for 
its activities through revenues from water, wastewater, or stormwater customers.  An authorized 
rate making process would be outlined in the enabling legislation.      
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 184 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Management - Governance 
 
5-21 
5.4.3  What are Some of the Challenges?  
Current challenges to providing safe drinking water and treat wastewater cost-effectively and 
handle polluted water runoff are many and varied.  Some of these challenges are: 
 
▪ Uncertain regulatory requirements that continue to evolve and address increasingly 
rigorous public health and environmental threats. 
 
▪ Need for increasingly more sophisticated and highly technical solutions.    
 
▪ Anticipated high costs to rehabilitate and/or replace aging water, sewer, and stormwater 
infrastructure. 
 
▪ Struggles to keep user rates at affordable levels for a small customer base which are 
adequate to sustain the utility service and keep it viable into the future.  
 
5.4.4  Is a Water Authority Better Positioned to Meet These Challenges?     
Some of the reasons and potential benefits of a Water Authority are:  
 
▪ As the Authority grows and serves more customers, there are opportunities to achieve 
economies of scale and enhanced purchase power.  
 
▪ An opportunity for an Authority to create water and sewer rates and policies that are more 
uniform and consistent throughout the County.  
 
▪ Better equipped to provide reliability, emergency preparedness, and sustainability for the 
utility systems. 
 
▪ Increased opportunities for flexibility in operations and sharing of resources.  
 
▪ A public agency solely focused and dedicated to water or wastewater or stormwater 
services. 
D R A F T

<!-- page 185 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
5-22 
 
Management - Governance 
 
▪ Ability to create partnerships or take over operation of infrastructure for the benefit of rural 
communities and outlying residential subdivisions and small Towns.   
 
5.4.5  Advantages of a Regional Water Authority 
▪ Minimal administration of supervisors. 
 
▪ Fewer support departments such as payroll, billing, HR, engineering, etc. 
 
▪ Better utilization of resources such as computers, staff, vehicles, specialty equipment. 
 
▪ Timely, uniform response of staff and equipment.  
 
▪ Back-up resources. 
 
▪ Less disruption due to employee or council turnover. 
 
▪ Long-term goals, plans, procedures, and policies are in place.  
 
▪ Better quality environmental impact. 
 
▪ Overall cost-savings. 
 
5.4.6  Can Municipalities Match the Services of a Regional Water Authority?  
Yes.  Municipalities that provide services to a larger customer base can match most of the 
advantages of a regional authority.  The City of Salisbury and City of Fruitland are two examples 
of municipalities that are providing excellent, high quality water and sewer utility service 
comparable to a Water Authority.

5.4.7 County/Regional Water Authority  
The degree of complexity or simplicity of the organization for a Water Authority will vary with 
the size of the customer base and number of facilities operated.   As the authority grows the 
organization chart will increasingly resemble a large corporation in form and structure.  
 
The basic functions of a Water Authority are:  
 
▪ Governance 
▪ Management 
▪ Administration 
▪ Finance  
▪ Operations 
▪ Maintenance 
▪ Construction 
 
Within these basic functions, a myriad of tasks would need to be accomplished: 
 
▪ Planning 
▪ Design and Plan Review  
▪ Permitting  
▪ Capital Improvement Budgeting 
▪ Construction Management  
▪ Legal  
▪ Land Acquisition  
▪ Billing and Collections  
▪ Accounting 
▪ Purchasing  
▪ IT 
▪ Human Resources  
▪ Customer Relations 
▪ Inspections 
▪ Monitoring And Reporting

5-24 
 
Management - Governance 
 
5.4.8 Governance 
The governance function would involve a board of directors or commissioners or trustees.  The 
board would be comprised of individuals with the interests, knowledge, and aptitude specific to 
water or sewer or stormwater infrastructure as well as a dedication to a public service under a 
business-model while protecting public health and the environment.  The board would typically 
be appointed by the political subdivisions being served by the authority.  This implies that a Water 
Authority could and should serve county residents both inside and outside municipal boundaries.  
The term of the board member would be set in the enabling legislation.    
 
5.4.9 Management  
The management function would necessitate the need for a General Manager or Chief Executive 
Officer or Executive to oversee the success of all the basic functions within the authority.  It would 
be a full-time position involved in all aspects of the authority.  The Manager would liaison with 
the governing board and legal counsel.  As an authority grows, it can require a full-time lawyer for 
legal services. 
 
5.4.10 Administration  
The administrative function not only provides clerical support, and also could encompass human 
resources, information technology, and customer services. 
 
5.4.11 Finance 
Included under this function would be the tasks associated with accounting, billing, collections, 
purchasing, payroll, account payable, etc.  
 
5.4.12 Operations  
Licensed water and wastewater superintendents would be hired to lead this function and direct the 
work of water treatment, water distribution, wastewater treatment and sewer collection operators 
handling the day-to-day tasks.  This size of this staff would grow in proportion to the number of 
customers served and number of facilities operated.   
 
D R A F T

Management - Governance 
 
5-25 
5.4.13 Maintenance 
The maintenance function includes electrician, mechanics, communications technicians, and  
whole host of other tradesman, which would be more economically subcontracted to private firms 
until the Water Authority grows larger than possibly envisioned for Wicomico County.   
 
5.4.14 Construction  
With an ongoing and self-sustaining Water Authority, the construction function would involve the 
planning, designing, constructing and inspection of water and sewer infrastructure expansion and 
renewal.  The construction would be undertaken by the Water Authority if some cases and by 
private developers in other cases.

Based on the number of households and recognizing the number of years and time horizon to build 
the infrastructure to connect these customers, the likelihood of a creating a separate and self-funded 
Water Authority appears highly improbable.  The numbers above are existing households and the 
number of households will increase in the future.  However, these areas are not expected to be 
high-growth urban areas.  These areas are “rural” in nature currently and future growth planning 
and land use planning will seek to maintain its rural character.    
 
Our research indicates that for a Water Authority to be a practical and cost-effective water utility 
provider, it would need service a larger customer base.  For a Water Authority to be practical and 
cost-effective, it would need to collaborate with some of the municipalities outside of the metro 
corridor.  If the County wishes to further explore this option, the logical next steps would involve 
an exploratory effort to complete a further evaluation of the number of municipalities willing to 
participate in the creation of a Water Authority.  If there is interest and a willingness to explore 
the creation of a Water Authority with the County and municipalities, the next study would identify 
the expected total customer base and estimated revenues and expenses in order to determine its 
potential to be a cost-effective solution. 
 
5.5  Regulated On-Site Areas  
A fourth management concept was developed within this Master Plan and given the title 
“Regulated Onsite Systems.”  The Regulated On-Site Areas proposed in this Master Plan are land 
D R A F T

<!-- page 190 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Management - Governance 
 
5-27 
parcels that have been subdivided and subject to MD-COMAR On-Site approvals for individual 
wells and septic disposal systems.  It is intended to conform with County Comprehensive Land 
Use Plan, limiting suburban growth in rural areas.  It applies to areas pre-determined to 
acknowledge that the environmental benefit of central sewers would not outweigh the costs to 
construct and operate the infrastructure.  Development in these areas would only be allowed 
subject to MD COMAR – approved individually owned on-site systems.   
 
This management concept basically applies to all Tier III areas (not converted to Tier II) and Tier 
IV areas shown in Appendix 6: Growth Tier of the 2017 County Comprehensive Plan.  With 
County approval of this management concept in this Master Plan, the next steps would require 
revisions to the Growth Tiers and approval by the Maryland Department of Planning.   
 
5.6  Recommendation 
Chapter 5 of the Master Plan under Management – Governance evaluated the continuance of the 
Urban Services Commission under County Code 97 in comparison to the creation of a semi-
governmental, semi-independent Water Authority versus a third alternative being the creation of a 
Water Division within the County’s Public Works Department.   
 
The Master Plan hereby recommends that the County legislatively revises County Code 97 for 
Urban Services to remove all provisions for water and sewer utility services and furthermore 
recommends the creation of a Water Division within County government under the Department of 
Public Works.   
 
Furthermore, the Master Plan recommends that the County implements a designated land-use 
category defining low density, rural and agricultural zoned areas to be “Regulated On-Site Areas” 
whereby all water and sewer comes under the purview of the Wicomico County Health 
Department.   
 
Figure 5-3 shows the envisioned jurisdictional organization structure breakdown for water and 
wastewater facilities in Wicomico County.

Chapter 6: Financial Management 
 
6-1 
Chapter 6:  Financial Management  
6.1  Introduction  
This Chapter of the Master Plan will discuss financial considerations associated with water and 
sewer infrastructure.  The cost information presented herein is planning level, based upon costs for 
the current 2021-2022 fiscal years.  In the future, as specific water and sewer projects are 
implemented, the cost estimates need to be revisited and adjusted for changes in project scope, 
size, and inflationary impacts.  A guiding principle and end goal of the financial considerations is 
the development and implementation of a sustainable water and sewer rate structure as well as 
relevant fees and assessments.  Financial sustainability in this Master Plan is intended to support 
the financial requirements to operate a utility system that optimizes environmental, economic, and 
social benefits.  This includes maintaining an adequate long-term reserve fund to pay off future 
repairs and replacements as the utility systems age.  This also includes appropriate “impact” fees 
when applicable to follow a guiding principle that “growth-pays-for-growth.”  
 
The financial considerations, funding capabilities, and rate structures will vary depending upon 
the type of management/governance structure selected and implemented by the County.  In 
Chapter 5, the recommended management/governance model was creation of a County Water 
Division under the Public Works Department.  As this Water Division grows and increases to serve 
a larger number of customers; it may necessitate becoming a separate, self-sustaining 
“Department” within County government.  In the following sections of Chapter 6, we will discuss 
and outline some of the distinguishing financial considerations related to an Urban Services 
approach versus a Water Authority or a Water Department.      
 
6.1.1 Urban Service Systems  
The County currently operates Urban Service utility systems in Fruitland, Salisbury, and Delmar.   
Some of these Urban Service areas are contiguous to municipal limits.  Others are surrounded by 
and within the boundary of the municipality and some, such as the recently created Morris Mill 
Water System, are fully outside the municipal limits.  In an “Urban Services” water or sewer utility 
system, the County acts as an intermediary.  From a financial perspective, the County needs to 
acquire funding capital for the infrastructure but will not need to fund the day-to-day operations, 
D R A F T

<!-- page 194 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
6-2 
 
Chapter 6: Financial Management 
maintenance, repair, or replacement.  The County will fund the underground infrastructure using 
County bonds, grants, and/or loans and collect the debt service for loan repayments or bond 
repayment for the term of the loan or bond.  The County would not operate the collection, 
distribution, or treatment systems.  As such, the County would not hire operations and maintenance 
staff, particularly licensed superintendents or operators, nor manage the human resources and 
employment benefits.  The rate structure, billing, and collections would be simplified.  Rates would 
be set based upon the fixed annual expense to pay back loans and bonds.  Eventually, these billings 
would cease when the loans and bonds are retired.  The individual County users connected to 
Urban Service systems would pay the users’ rates imposed by the municipality for the operation 
and maintenance expenses generally referred to as the “service charge.”  In most cases, the service 
charge imposed by the municipality will be higher than the actual expenses incurred, typically 
about 50% above actual incurred expenses.  At the present time, all municipalities within the 
County no longer intend to support Urban Service Districts contiguous or near corporate limits.  
Accordingly, no further discussion of these financial considerations is necessary.  
 
 6.1.2 Water Authority  
Financial considerations for the County adopting a Water Authority management-governance 
approach are different from an Urban Services.  A Water Authority would not have the same access 
to General Obligation Bonds and be required to use Revenue Bonds, which often a have higher 
interest rate.    
 
A Water Authority, compared to an Urban Services, would need significantly more upfront money 
from the County for its formation, staffing, administrative facilities, and start-up.    
 
A Water Authority would have similar capabilities to a County Water Division or Department 
relative to applying for and receiving grant and loan funding from either State or Federal sources.   
 
Once established, revenue requirements would come solely from the customers being provided 
water and/or sewer service.  There a few reasons a Water Authority may be more likely to keep 
rates at sustainable (higher) levels compared to municipal systems.  First, a Water Authority is not 
subject to regular public election which often creates a strong negative incentive to maintain higher 
D R A F T

<!-- page 195 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
Chapter 6: Financial Management 
 
6-3 
rates needed to offset expenses.  Second, a Water Authority does not have direct access to a 
municipalities’ general funds to offset deficits.    
 
On the other hand, a Water Authority is more dependent on a large customer base and an economy 
of scale to drive down the unit cost per customer to operate at user rates that are affordable.    
 
6.1.3 Water Division or Department  
Financial considerations for the County Government providing public water and sewer utility 
service under a Water Division or Department would involve the creation and management of an 
enterprise fund, separate from the County’s general fund.  All revenues required to cover costs and 
expenses would come solely from the customers being provided the water and sewer services.    
There is a potential for the Water Division to utilize the resources of other County departments, 
such as accounting, purchasing, and human resources that may currently be underutilized.   
 
6.2  Funding Capital Infrastructure MDE  
Wicomico County will need to make large capital investments at the outset to construct the water 
and wastewater infrastructure being proposed in the Master Plan.  This is not unusual or unique as 
water and sewer infrastructure requires large capital expenditures.  As these utilities are protecting 
public health and the environment, they are investments in the public interest.  Grant funding is 
preferred over loan funding for obvious reasons.  There are justifications for State and Federal 
grant programs.  State and Federal grants help communities meet obligations unilaterally imposed 
by State and Federal programs, laws, and initiatives.  Loans from State and Federal sources are 
often available at below-market rates.  The infrastructure projects proposed in this Master Plan are 
very typical of the kind of project that qualifies for State and Federal funding.   
 
The leading sources of funding from the State of Maryland come under the MDE and include the 
following as shown in Table 6-1.  It should be noted that the MDE revolving loan program receives 
money from the Federal Government through EPA on an annual basis in order to grow its size and 
reach to fund water and sewer infrastructure.  Other sources of funds from Maryland are driven by 
economic incentives such as the Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) program

6.3  Funding Capital Infrastructure USDA 
Funding from the Federal Government is through the United States Department of Agriculture 
(USDA) Rural Development and its Water & Waste Disposal Loan & Grant Program.  It is 
applicable for rural areas and towns with populations of 10,000 or less.  Accordingly, Wicomico 
County rural villages, subdivision communities, and towns are eligible.  The City of Fruitland is 
eligible with its population below 10,000.   The City of Salisbury, with a population above 10,000, 
would not be eligible.  The loans from USDA are considered long-term and extend to 40 years; 
this is practical for buried infrastructure which usually has a service life beyond 50 years.  The 
interest rates are fixed and are based on the project need and median household income to achieve 
sustainable revenues and affordable user rates.  Partnerships with state, local, private, and non-
profit entities are encouraged.  Applications for these funds are accepted year-round, through the 
D R A F T

<!-- page 197 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
Chapter 6: Financial Management 
 
6-5 
local Rural Development (RD) office.  For Wicomico County, the local RD office is in Dover, 
Delaware.  Wicomico County used USDA grant and loan funds for the construction or the central 
water system for the subdivisions in the Morris Mill area.   
 
6.4  FY 2021- 2023 MDE Funding Applications  
In January 2021, GMB recommended Wicomico County submit applications to MDE to determine 
priority ranking and if projects qualify for grant or loan funds under the Water Quality State 
Revolving Fund (WQSRF) for three (3) potential service areas.  The purpose of the applications 
for wastewater collection and treatment systems was to gather information for the County in 
assessing the potential of State grant and/or loan assistance.  At the end of June 2021, MDE 
published its draft Project Priority List.  The applications were focused on three (3) potential 
service areas as follows: 
 
▪ Mardela Springs  
▪ Whitehaven  
▪ Eastside Corridor (along Route 346). 
 
In Mardela Springs and Whitehaven, applications were submitted separately for the sewer 
collection system and for the treatment/disposal system for a total of four (4) applications.  For the 
Eastside Corridor service area only one (1) application for sewer collection was submitted.  MDE 
received a total of 141 applications.  Wicomico County applications were ranked as follows: 
 
▪ Ranked #6:   
Mardela Springs Sewer System 
▪ Ranked #7: 
Eastside Corridor Sewer Extension 
▪ Ranked #30: 
Whitehaven Sewer System  
▪ Ranked #67 
Mardela Springs Treatment Plant  
▪ Ranked #102  
Whitehaven WWTP 
 
The rankings show that these Wicomico County projects would be received favorably for funding 
support.  MDE will set aside Fiscal Years 21-23 funding for the forty-six (46) highest ranked

While these projects received high rankings based upon need, protection of water quality, and

6.5  Project Cost Estimates  
Preliminary project cost estimates were prepared for ten (10) service areas identified in Chapter 3.    
These ten (10) areas represent Rural Villages and subdivision communities that were not expected 
to connect to a nearby incorporated City or Town.  For seven (7) service areas adjacent to 
municipalities having existing water and sewer infrastructure and for two (2) community areas; 
namely, Delmar East and East Wicomico, preliminary cost estimates were not prepared. 
 
The Delmar East and East Wicomico proposed community service areas are designated as Town 
Transition zones under the County Land Use map, but they lack a nucleus of houses or significant 
number of existing customers to support the construction of the infrastructure in the near future.   
Cost estimates prepared under this Master Plan would not only be too outdated to be useful, but 
would also be ill-defined and inadequate in size and scope.    
  
Preliminary cost estimates were not prepared for services areas subject to municipal annexation as 
a precondition for water and sewer utility due to the complexity of quantifying the unique financial 
arrangements associated with impact fees and upgrades required to existing collection and 
treatment plants.    
 
The preliminary estimates were based upon 2nd quarter calendar year 2021 costs and pricing.  Use 
of these estimates in the future will need to account for inflationary indexing.  Because these 
estimates are “planning level” and not based on “preliminary engineering,” the construction 
estimates include a contingency factor of 25%.  The non-construction related costs associated with 
the project include administration, legal, land acquisition, financing, engineering, etc. and were 
based upon typical industry percentages for these categories.  In particular, the engineering fee

costs are admittedly very speculative in this Master Plan, and they are highly influenced by the 
amount of grant money received from State and Federal sources.  The assumed grant amount is

factors incorporated into Table 6-3, the takeaway message is an expectation that the annual user 
charges for central sewer utility service will be a monthly cost around $100, which in-turn results 
in an annual expense of around $1,200.

<!-- page 222 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 7: Implementation 
 
7-1 
Chapter 7:  Implementation 
7.1  Introduction   
This Chapter presents recommended actions to be undertaken by Wicomico County to accomplish 
the principles and objectives of the Master Plan for Water and Sewer.  After review and approval 
of the Master Plan by the Department of Public Works and the office of the County Executive, it 
will be presented for acceptance by the County Council. Additionally, the municipalities and the 
public will need to understand the guiding principles.  
 
Implementation of the concepts and recommended actions will follow multiple paths and involve 
various entities and departments within County Government.  On the local County level, these 
entities include the following: 
 
▪ County Council 
▪ County Executive’s Office  
▪ Department of Public Works 
▪ Department of Planning and Zoning  
▪ Wicomico County Health Department 
▪ Municipalities  
▪ Rural Villages 
▪ Subdivision Communities  
 
On the State level, implementation will require involvement and support from the following: 
 
▪ Maryland Department of Planning  
o Septic’s Law Implementation (Tier Maps) 
o County Water and Sewerage Facilities Plan 
o Priority Funding Areas 
o Coast Smart  
 
▪ Maryland Department of Environment  
o Wastewater Permits 
D R A F T

<!-- page 223 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
7-2 
 
Chapter 7: Implementation 
o Wetlands and Waterways Program 
o Water Supply 
o Water Quality 
o Water Quality Trading  
o Chesapeake Bay TMDL 
o MD Phase III Watershed Implementation Plan  
o Water Quality Financing 
o Bay Restoration Funding 
 
The Master Plan examined the three (3) major components of water and sewer utility infrastructure 
services.  They were: (1) management/governance structure, (2) technological aspects, and (3) 
financial considerations.  Implementation steps for each of these component elements will be 
presented to meet the goals of the Master Plan and be compatible with the guiding principles 
adopted. 
 
7.2  Guiding Principles and Implementation Action 
Following in Table 7-1 is a listing of the guiding principles to be adopted by Wicomico County 
by acceptance of the Master Plan.

7.3  Management – Governance  
Chapter 5 of the Master Plan investigated alternative management-governance approaches 
including continuation of Urban Services Commission under Chapter 97 of the County Code, 
creation of a Water Authority with a separate Board and independent powers, and the creation of 
a Water Division or Department within the current County government structure.  The 
implementation actions in this area are as follows: 
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 225 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
7-4 
 
Chapter 7: Implementation 
▪ County Council adopt a platform committed to the proposition that County 
Government will provide central water and/or sewer utility services to County residents 
where there is public health and environmental protection issues that cannot be more 
effectively served in the public interest by existing municipal utility systems or 
expansion thereof.  
 
▪ Adopt recommended management structure for the creation of a Water Division to 
implement the goals, objectives, and strategies for water and sewer utility services in 
the County. 
 
▪ Enact enabling legislation as needed to carry out the Master Plan, such as: 
 
o Modification to Chapter 97 of the County Code to eliminate or diminish the use 
of Urban Services Districts for Water and Sewer utility services. 
 
o Adopt ordinance(s) for the prohibition of private wastewater systems serving 
more than one land parcel and determine the long-term status of the one (1) 
existing private wastewater system in the County. 
 
o Adopt ordinance(s), if needed, for the continuation of the current policy 
allowing private drinking water utility systems in the County.  
 
o Amend the 2010 Wicomico County Water and Sewerage Plan to incorporate 
the water and sewer utility system projects recommended and proposed. 
 
o Revisit and revise the Tier Maps in collaboration with the municipalities having 
existing utility systems in accordance with the 2012 Septic’s Law. 
 
o Adopt budget items and funding to support the creation county water and sewer 
utility systems. 
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 226 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 7: Implementation 
 
7-5 
▪ Establish and maintain communication with all municipalities having water and sewer 
utility systems and define how annexations of unincorporated areas will be 
implemented for the delivery of new water and sewer service.  
 
▪ Establish an organizational chart of the Water Division within the Department of Public 
Works. 
 
▪ Begin now the process of hiring the Water Division leader and assembling the 
supporting project management and administrative staff.  
 
▪ Establish furnished office spaces and equipment to integrate the Water Division within 
the Department of Public Works and the overall county government inclusive of human 
resources, financial, information technology, purchasing, etc.  
 
▪ Review and update land-use goals, growth and water resource objectives provided in 
the 2017 Comprehensive Plan for the provisions of central water and/or sewer systems 
proposed herein.   
 
▪ Implement public awareness and information strategies to communicate with the public 
and solicit valuable input to shape and refine the planning and design of the proposed 
central utility service projects.    
 
7.4  Water and Sewer Technical Issues 
The implementation action items resulting from the proposed establishment of sewer and water 
utility services by the County include the following:  
 
▪ Apply for and secure clean water discharge permits from the State of Maryland for 
groundwater discharge, inclusive of site selection and hydrogeological studies, for the 
following proposed service areas: 
 
o Mardela Springs 
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 227 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
7-6 
 
Chapter 7: Implementation 
o Whitehaven Village 
 
o Parsonsburg Village  
 
▪ Apply for and secure clean water discharge permits for surface water discharge under 
the Federal NPDES Program (National Pollutant Discharge Elimination System) 
administered by MDE, inclusive of outfall site selection and water quality modeling 
and testing studies, for the following proposed service areas: 
 
o Mardela Springs 
 
o Whitehaven Village 
 
o Parsonsburg Village 
 
o Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin Villages 
 
o Coulbourne Mill Subdivision Community  
 
▪ Develop preliminary feasibility/engineering reports to closely define utility service 
area, expected number of initial customers, choose initial system capacity, and 
preliminary project and user charge cost estimates for the following proposed service 
areas: 
 
o Mardela Springs  
 
o Whitehaven Village 
 
o Parsonsburg Village  
 
o Nanticoke-Bivalve-Tyaskin Villages  
 
o Coulbourne Mill Subdivision Community  
 
7.5  Financial 
Finding the money for the big capital investment needed to construct water and sewer 
infrastructure is a daunting task, but not impossible or unfeasible.  It initially begins with relatively 
small budget amounts for developing in-house capacity, master planning, and development of an 
D R A F T

<!-- page 228 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
Chapter 7: Implementation 
 
7-7 
implementation plan.  It then proceeds to preliminary feasibility and engineering reports combined 
with associated scientific and environmental investigations, followed by application and 
acquisition of necessary funding resources to construct the water and sewer infrastructure.  The 
last step is the successful start-up, operation, and maintenance of the infrastructure based upon a 
sustainable user revenue support system.   
 
The recommended financial implementation action measures are as follows: 
 
▪ Submit funding applications to State and Federal agencies seeking the maximum 
amount of grants funds available for the planning, design, construction, and operation 
of water and sewer utility infrastructure.  Potential sources of grant funds come for 
various programs with mission objectives that support, public health protection, 
environmental protection, economic growth, and development of rural communities.   
A good example would be the USDA – Rural Development Search Grant program, 
funded with 100% Grant in the fixed amount of $30,000.   
 
▪ Seek loan and bond funds with low interest rates and extended terms to minimize debt 
service payments in the initial years when the customer base is the smallest.  
 
▪ Develop an internal financial accounting and management plan and program to track 
all water and sewer infrastructure spending collectively and/or individually that will 
initially be funded under the County general fund and eventually converted to 
enterprise funds. 
 
▪ Fund ongoing annual budget amounts in the Annual County Capital Improvement Plan 
under the Department of Public Works dedicated to the potential infrastructure projects 
recommended in the Master Plan.  
 
▪ Convert the Capital Improvement infrastructure funding under the Department of 
Public Works to “Water & Sewer Enterprise Funds” as soon as projects become 
operational and customer user bills are issued.    
D R A F T

<!-- page 229 -->
WICOMICO  COUNTY  WATER  &  SEWER  MASTER  PLAN 
 
7-8 
 
Chapter 7: Implementation 
 
▪ Develop and implement financial strategies applicable to “user charge” systems to 
account for debt service payments, ongoing service charges, impact fees, and 
expansions fees, etc. that best serve the interests of Wicomico County citizens and the 
individual county customers being served by water and sewer infrastructure.   
 
▪ Develop financial strategies to define and allocate costs associated with the adopted 
policy of “Growth Pays for Growth” under the most practical, workable, and equitable 
arrangement.  
 
7.6  Schedule  
The following activities and time frames recommended to implement the Wicomico County Water 
& Sewer Master Plan are shown in Table 7-2.   The schedule of activities is shown for the next 
two (2) years (2021 to 2023), at which time the schedule should be updated, revised, and reissued 
for the next two (2) years (2023 to 2025).

WICOMICO COUNTY 
 WATER & SEWER MASTER PLAN (WC-WSMP) 
 
Town of Mardela Springs Meeting 
GMB File No. 190079 
 
August 17, 2021 
7:00 p.m. 
 
 
A meeting with the Town of Mardela Springs was held on Tuesday, August 17, 2021, at 
7:00 p.m., at the Mardela Springs Town Hall during a monthly Commissioners meeting.  
Those in attendance were: 
 
Name 
Organization 
 
Stanford Robinson 
 
Mardela Springs Commissioner 
Angie Drehmer 
Mardela Springs Commissioner 
Robert Walter 
Mardela Springs Commissioner 
Mike Murphy 
Mardela Springs Commissioner 
   
Debbie Bailey 
Mardela Springs Commissioner 
Kortney Robinson 
Clerk 
Robert Alan VanMeter 
Code Enforcer 
Robert Benson 
Town of Mardela Springs Attorney 
 
Paul Wilber,  
Wicomico County Attorney Representative  
Peter Bozick Jr., P.E. 
GMB 
 
Katherine McAllister, P.E. 
GMB 
 
 
Items of Discusison 
 
1. GMB provided an overview on the purpose and goals for the WC-WSMP.  The 
Purpose statement was reviewed and is attached to these minutes.   
   
2. The objective of the WC-WSMP is to evaluate areas throughout the County and 
determine alternatives for providing safe drinking water and sewer to all residents 
throughout the County.  During the initial phase of this study, the service areas being 
evaluated coincide with growth areas from the 2017 Wicomico County 
Comprehensive Plan Land Use Map.  This map is attached for reference.  
 
3. The County is meeting with all municipalities within the County to provide an overview 
of the work to be completed.  The focus is on existing homes and properties.  County 
will also review areas of existing failing septic systems.   
 
4. Overall there are approximately 35,000 homes in County.  Half of those (17,500 
homes) have central water and sewer while the other half (17500 homes) have well 
and septic.   
 
D R A F T

<!-- page 234 -->
WC-WSMP: Town of Mardela Springs Minutes 
August 17, 2021 
Page 2 
 
 
 
5. The Town of Mardela Springs does not have any existing public sewer or public water 
infrastructure. 
 
6. Mardela Springs completed a study several years ago to review alternatives and 
estimate cost associated with installation of public water and/or sewer throughout the 
Town.   Due to the high costs the project was not feasible and the burden on the 
residents would have been unfeasible due to the costs.  
 
7. Town noted they have a number of vacant homes where collecting bills becomes a 
major challenge.     
 
8. GMB discussed potential project scenarios that could include a partnership between 
the Town and County.  For example, the County could install public sewer and public 
water to serve the Town and neighboring parcels.  The County would own and 
maintain the public infrastructure and charge residents based on usage in order to 
maintain the system.  The goal of all future projects would be to provide a win-win 
situation for all involved while providing the necessary water/sewer utilities to 
maintain public health.   
 
9. 
One of the main concerns expressed by the Town is affordability.  GMB noted if the 
Master Plan is adopted by the County the next goal would be to prioritize projects 
and then work with State and Federal agencies to determine funding and grant 
opportunities.  At this time, it is difficult to determine the amount of grants and final 
user rates that would be associated with this project.   
 
10. Discussed regional versus individual sewer plant alternatives for Mardela Springs.  
At this time, it is anticipated a sewer plant would serve Mardela Springs and 
surrounding community only.  Extending sewer from Hebron or Sharptown is a 
possibility but has not been reviewed in detail at this time.  
 
11. Well water is not as much a concern for residents in the areas as septic issues.   
 
12. The Town expressed concerns regarding the maintenance of a public system and 
verification that the County would maintain the system indefinitely without placing the 
burden on the Town after it is constructed.  The Town does not have the resources 
to maintain a public sewer system.  
 
13. Discussed economic aspects of providing dependable public water and sewer which 
in turn brings increased economic growth.   
       
14. The WC-WSMP will be looking at providing County wide water and sewer and the 
alternatives for developing a governing entity to manage this infrastructure.  This 
governing structure is not known at this time but could be County sponsored, 
Regional Authority, Interjurisdictional agreements, or Privately sponsored.   
 
15. At this time, the County is looking at the different service areas and would like to 
develop several pilot projects.  The County could facilitate finding funding sources for 
D R A F T

<!-- page 235 -->
WC-WSMP: Town of Mardela Springs Minutes 
August 17, 2021 
Page 3 
 
 
 
completing infrastructure projects within certain service areas and partner with 
municipalities where possible.  The project goal would be to expand water and sewer 
services to unserved areas.   
 
a. Higher priority may be focused in areas where failing septic systems are known.   
b. In addition, areas where developed parcels have smaller lots (less than 1 acre) 
and older septic systems are a concern because as a septic system ages and 
fails, there is no additional land area for a replacement system.   
 
16. It was discussed that as septic tanks are taken offline, the County could apply 
applicable credits to their existing MS4 permits under MDE Phase III Watershed 
Implementation Plan. 
      
17. The Town of Mardela Springs would be interested in discussing this study further as 
the County moves forward.  Future meetings will be scheduled as progress 
continues.   
    
Please let us know if you see the need for any corrections or revisions.   
 
 
 
Respectfully Submitted, 
 
 
 
Katherine J. McAllister, P.E.

WICOMICO COUNTY 
WATER & SEWER  
MASTER PLAN  
P-1 
 
PURPOSE STATEMENT  
 
PURPOSE STATEMENT 
 
The objective of the Wicomico County – Water and Sewer Master Plan (WC-WSMP) is to outline a 
technical, managerial, and financial approach/policy for adoption by Wicomico County to provide safe 
drinking water and clean water discharge for all residents of Wicomico County in the form of a Master Plan 
document.  No less important is the safeguarding of public health, protection against degradation of our 
water environment, and the enrichment of the economic well-being for the Citizens of Wicomico County.  
The effort entails evaluating safe drinking water and sewer service capacities and capabilities of public 
(municipalities) and private utility providers, as well as individual onsite systems within Wicomico County 
and document both the available capacities as well as capacity deficiencies, leading toward practical and 
sustainable solutions.   
The long-term goal is to have viable safe drinking water and clean water (wastewater) utility system(s) 
serving all residents of Wicomico County, while the short-term goal is to identify and prioritize 
foundational projects and actions and potential funding sources to implement the Master Plan.  
A graphical representation of the purpose statement shows the overlapping and interdependent relationships 
of the technical infrastructure component relative to the managerial organization and the financial 
component to achieve a viable and sustainable water and wastewater utility system.  
 
 
Water & Sewer Infrastructure 
• 
Physical Infrastructure – Pipelines, Pump Stations, Treatment Plants  
• 
Operational Excellence – Human resources aided by technological advances  
• 
Proactive and cost-effective maintenance of existing infrastructure  
• 
Sustainable construction of new and improved infrastructure 
 
Governing Entity  
• 
County Sponsored, Separate Regional Authority, Interjurisdictional Agreements and/or Privately Sponsored 
under the Public Service Commission  
• 
Deep understanding of the fundamental nature and critical role of water and wastewater utility systems to serve 
communities and County residents 
• 
Understanding long-term needs to efficiently and effectively own, operate, maintain and renew utility systems  
• 
Effectiveness for prioritizing and implementing critical infrastructure projects  
 
Financial Management  
• 
Capability and Capacity to generate revenue commensurate with the needs for infrastructure construction, 
maintenance, operations and renewal/replacement without undue dependence on grant funds.   
Water & Sewer 
Infrastructure
Financial 
Management
Governing 
Entity

Items of Discusison 
1.
Wicomico County and GMB provided an overview to The Town of Willards on the
purpose and goals for the WC-WSMP.  The Purpose statement was reviewed and
is attached to these minutes.
2.
The objective of the WC-WSMP is to evaluate areas throughout the County and
determine alternatives for providing safe drinking water and sewer to all residents
throughout the County.  During the initial phase of this study the service areas
being evaluated coincide with growth areas from the 2017 Wicomico County
Comprehensive plan Land Use Map.  This map is attached for reference.
3.
The County is meeting with all municipalities within the County to provide an
overview of the work to be completed.
4.
Existing Town planning documents and water and sewer infrastructure capacity
were reviewed.
a.
Wastewater: Biological Nutrient Removal plant rated at 200,000 GPD.
b.
Water:
i.
Three (3) wells each rated at 160 gpm.
ii.
Two (2) elevated water towers, each 125,000-gallon capacity.
iii.
Water Plant rated at 250,000 gpd.
5.
Based on existing users and infill within the Town’s municipal boundary and the
above utility capacities, the Town is limited on additional capacity.  Therefore, the
D R A F T

<!-- page 240 -->
WC-WSMP: The Town of Willards Meeting Minutes 
September 3, 2019 
Page 2 
Town does not intend to annex or serve additional parcels surrounding the 
municipal boundary at this time.    
6.
If a parcel was to request service outside of the existing municipal boundary, the
Town’s policy would be to annex any parcels with Town utility services.  Cost
associated with extending the water and sewer infrastructure is at the cost of the
landowner.  The Town has not approved Urban Services in the past.
7.
With the existing rate structures and loans from past water and wastewater facility
upgrades, the Town is not in a position to take on additional debt.
8.
Discussed the fact that smaller Town’s often struggle in order to balance yearly
budgets because the water and sewer fees collected do not cover the high
operation and maintenance cost of operating sewer and water facilities in addition
to developing reserve funds for future needs with repairs and upgrades.  The
economy of scale with smaller communities is not there to offset high cost to run
the facilities.
9.
The WC-WSMP will be looking at providing County wide water and sewer and the
alternatives for developing a governing entity to manage this infrastructure.  This
governing structure is not known at this time but could be County sponsored,
Regional Authority, Interjurisdictional agreements or Privately sponsored.
10. At this time the County is looking at the different service areas and would like to
develop several pilot projects.  The County could facilitate finding funding sources
for completing infrastructure projects within a certain service areas and partner
with municipalities where possible.  The project goal would be to expand water
and sewer services to unserved areas.
a.
Higher priority may be focused in areas where failing septic systems are
known.
b.
In addition, areas where developed parcels have smaller lots (less than 1
acre) and older septic systems are a concern because as a septic system
ages and fails there is no additional land area for a replacement system.
11. Discussed the service area for The Town of Willards which incorporates the Town
of Pittsville and “Town Transition” areas between the two (2) municipalities.
12. As septic tanks are taken offline, associated nutrient loads could be available to
expand existing wastewater facilities or build new facilities.
13. Discussed pilot projects which incorporate existing facilities will need to address
the fact that municipal communities and their citizens have incurred debt over the
years to pay for these existing facilities.  The municipalities and its citizens may
need to be compensated accordingly.

County moves forward.  Future meetings will be scheduled as progress continues.

PURPOSE STATEMENT 
 
The  objective  of  the  Wicomico  County  –  Water  and  Sewer  Master  Plan  (WC‐WSMP)  is  to  outline  a  technical, 
managerial, and financial approach/policy for adoption by Wicomico County to provide safe drinking water and clean 
water discharge for all residents of Wicomico County in the form of a Master Plan document.  No less important is 
the safeguarding of public health, protection against degradation of our water environment, and the enrichment of 
the economic well‐being for the Citizens of Wicomico County.  
The  effort  entails  evaluating  safe  drinking  water  and  sewer  service  capacities  and  capabilities  of  public 
(municipalities)  and  private  utility  providers,  as  well  as  individual  onsite  systems  within  Wicomico  County  and 
document both the available capacities as well as capacity deficiencies, leading toward practical and sustainable 
solutions.   
The long‐term goal is to have viable safe drinking water and clean water (wastewater) utility system(s) serving all 
residents of Wicomico County, while the short‐term goal is to identify and prioritize foundational projects and actions 
and potential funding sources to implement the Master Plan.  
A graphical representation of the purpose statement shows the overlapping and interdependent relationships of the 
technical infrastructure component relative to the managerial organization and the financial component to achieve 
a viable and sustainable water and wastewater utility system.  
 
Water & Sewer Infrastructure 
 
Physical Infrastructure – Pipelines, Pump Stations, Treatment Plants  
 
Operational Excellence – Human resources aided by technological advances  
 
Proactive and cost‐effective maintenance of existing infrastructure  
 
Sustainable construction of new and improved infrastructure 
 
Governing Entity  
 
County  Sponsored,  Separate  Regional  Authority,  Interjurisdictional  Agreements  and/or  Privately  Sponsored 
under the Public Service Commission  
 
Deep understanding of the fundamental nature and critical role of water and wastewater utility systems to serve 
communities and County residents 
 
Understanding long‐term needs to efficiently and effectively own, operate, maintain and renew utility systems  
 
Effectiveness for prioritizing and implementing critical infrastructure projects  
 
Financial Management  
 
Capability and Capacity to generate revenue commensurate with the needs for infrastructure construction, 
maintenance, operations and renewal/replacement without undue dependence on grant funds.

Town of Delmar Meeting 
GMB File No. 190079 
September 11, 2019 
2:30 P.M. 
A meeting with the Town of Delmar was held on Wednesday, September 11, 2019, 
at 2:30 p.m., in the GMB Conference Room.  Those in attendance were: 
Name 
Organization 
 Email 
Sara Bynum-King 
Town of Delmar
